The observed scales are consistent with the proved first- and second-order
inverse errors (the last column is accounted for by the explicit
$x_0^{-2}$ term of \eqref{fce:kk:eq:inversefive}). Numerical agreement is a useful implementation check,
not a substitute for the asymptotic proof.

\subsection{What the computations do not certify}
The package does not verify a Lean formalization, prove the guessed
A215570 recurrence (source~75-03's program checks the certificate of
Part~\ref{fce:kk:part}, Section~\ref{fce:kk:sec:checks}), find the minimal
recurrence for any higher row, or
supply interval-certified decimal enclosures for every kernel root.
Its exact tests validate finite combinatorial and algebraic calculations.
Its root computations and asymptotic tables are high-precision numerical
diagnostics. The infinite statements are supported by the mathematical
proofs above.

\part{The convolution route, general step sets and the shape of an excursion}\label{fce:lg:part}

This Part prints the results of source~42 that source~61 does not
contain, and source~42's proofs of shared results where they follow a
genuinely different route. It uses the merged notation of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:notation}; Table~\ref{fce:tab:renamed} lists every
symbol of source~42 that was renamed. Statements proved in both sources
are printed once, in Part~I, and are only cited here.

\section{Labelled steps and the log-excursion series}\label{fce:lg:sec:labelled}
\subsection{Labelled steps and composition}
For a finite list of integer steps $\boldsymbol s=(s_1,\ldots,s_q)$ having
at least one negative and one positive entry, write
\[
 \mathcal H=\{\boldsymbol k\in\N^q:\boldsymbol s\cdot\boldsymbol k=0\},\qquad
 |\boldsymbol k|=k_1+\cdots+k_q.
\]
An excursion is a word in the \emph{labelled} steps whose partial sums are
nonnegative and whose final sum is zero. Distinct labels remain distinct
even when their numerical steps coincide. Let $e_{\boldsymbol k}$ count
excursions with composition $\boldsymbol k$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Egf}
 \E(\boldsymbol x)=\sum_{\boldsymbol k\in\mathcal H}e_{\boldsymbol k}
 \boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k},\qquad e_{\boldsymbol0}=1.
\end{equation}
For the rows, $q=r$ and the steps are \eqref{fce:eq:steps}; the encoding
of Section~\ref{fce:sec:encoding} turns the prefix condition into this
nonnegative convention without changing the balanced composition, and
\eqref{fce:eq:diag} expresses $A_r(n)$ as a diagonal coefficient.

Lemma~\ref{fce:lem:kernel} holds for every such step list (source~42
states it in this generality): put $h=-\min_i s_i$, $h'=\max_i s_i$ and
$x_-=\sum_{i:s_i=-h}x_i$, and replace $x_1$ by $x_-$ in
\eqref{fce:eq:rootproduct}. The proof given there applies verbatim,
because $u^h(1-tP_{\boldsymbol x}(u))$ evaluated at $u=0$ is $-tx_-$, and so
do both arguments given there for removing the auxiliary variable. In
particular $\E(\boldsymbol x)$ is algebraic over $\Q(\boldsymbol x)$.
Generically the subset-product construction gives the finite degree bound
$\binom{h+h'}{h}$, although the actual degree can be smaller.

\subsection{The log-excursion series}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Bformal}
 \Lcal(\boldsymbol x)=\sum_{\substack{\boldsymbol k\in\mathcal H\\|\boldsymbol k|>0}}
 b^0_{\boldsymbol k}\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k},\qquad
 b^0_{\boldsymbol k}=\frac1{|\boldsymbol k|}\binom{|\boldsymbol k|}{k_1,\ldots,k_q}.
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{fce:lem:bridge} and its proof, which uses only the kernel
lemma, hold in this generality; in source~42's form the lemma reads
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:exp}
 \E(\boldsymbol x)=\exp\Lcal(\boldsymbol x),\qquad
 \Lcal(t\boldsymbol x)=\ct_u\bigl[-\log(1-tP_{\boldsymbol x}(u))\bigr];
\end{equation}
this is source~42's form of the bridge--excursion identity, and its own
proof of it is printed after Lemma~\ref{fce:lem:bridge} as a second proof.
The factor $1/|\boldsymbol k|$ is crucial. Replacing $\Lcal$ by the
unnormalized bridge series would give a false identity.

\section{A directional convolution theorem}\label{fce:lg:sec:convolution}
This section contains the elementary analytic mechanism behind the leading
equivalent. It avoids assuming finite moments of the bridge-series mass;
those moments generally diverge.

\subsection{Critical multinomial weights}
Fix positive rational numbers $p_1,\ldots,p_q$ with
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:p}
 \sum_i p_i=1,\qquad \sum_i p_i s_i=0,
 \qquad \sum_i p_i s_i^2>0.
\end{equation}
Let $N$ tend to infinity through integers for which
$\boldsymbol k_N=N\boldsymbol p$ is integral. Define
\[
 b_{\boldsymbol k}=b^0_{\boldsymbol k}\boldsymbol p^{\boldsymbol k}
 \quad(\boldsymbol k\in\mathcal H\setminus\{0\}),
 \qquad b_{\boldsymbol0}=0,
 \qquad \Bmass=\sum_{\boldsymbol k}b_{\boldsymbol k}.
\]
Here and below sequences indexed off $\mathcal H$ are extended by zero.
Put $\varsigma=(q+1)/2$.

\begin{lemma}[Mass, bounds, and fixed shifts]\label{fce:lg:lem:bounds}
The mass $\Bmass$ is finite and positive. There are constants $c,C>0$ such
that
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:bounds}
 b_{\boldsymbol k}\le C|\boldsymbol k|^{-\varsigma}\quad(|\boldsymbol k|>0),\qquad
 b_{\boldsymbol k_N}\sim
 \frac{N^{-\varsigma}}{(2\pi)^{(q-1)/2}\sqrt{p_1\cdots p_q}}.
\end{equation}
For each fixed $\boldsymbol\ell\in\mathcal H$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:shift}
 \frac{b_{\boldsymbol k_N-\boldsymbol\ell}}{b_{\boldsymbol k_N}}\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
The same conclusions along the target ray hold after any fixed admissible
shift of that target.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $Y$ take value $s_i$ with probability $p_i$. Summing by length gives
\[
 \Bmass=\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{\Prob(Y_1+\cdots+Y_m=0)}m.
\]
The return probability is $O(m^{-1/2})$. One direct proof uses the
Fourier integral of the characteristic function. Its modulus equals one at
only finitely many points on a period, because the step distribution is
nonconstant. Near each such point its modulus is at most $1-c\theta^2$,
after translating the point, and elsewhere it is bounded away from one.
The Gaussian integral bounds the result by $Cm^{-1/2}$. The positive and
negative steps and rational probabilities ensure a nonempty zero-sum word,
so $\Bmass>0$.

For the uniform pointwise bound in \eqref{fce:lg:eq:bounds}, the maximum
point mass of a fixed $q$-category multinomial distribution is
$O(m^{-(q-1)/2})$. To verify this directly, when every
$k_i\ge p_i m/2$, Stirling's bounds give the stated power times
$\exp(-mD_{\mathrm{KL}}(\boldsymbol k/m\Vert\boldsymbol p))\le1$. Here
$D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\boldsymbol\varpi\Vert\boldsymbol p)
=\sum_i \varpi_i\log(\varpi_i/p_i)$ is relative entropy, with $0\log0=0$.
On the complementary closed subset of the probability simplex the relative
entropy is bounded below by a positive constant. Factorial bounds, or the
multinomial theorem followed by that entropy bound, give an exponentially
small term, which is smaller than the required power. Dividing by $m$
proves the first bound. Applying Stirling at $\boldsymbol k_N=N\boldsymbol p$
proves its asymptotic form.

Finally, with $L=|\boldsymbol\ell|$, the ratio in \eqref{fce:lg:eq:shift} is
exactly
\[
 \frac{(N-L-1)!}{(N-1)!}
 \prod_i\frac{(Np_i)!}{(Np_i-\ell_i)!}\,p_i^{-\ell_i},
\]
which tends to one. Fixed shifts change none of the preceding power bounds
along the ray.
\end{proof}

\subsection{One large summand}
For sequences on $\N^q$, let $*$ denote additive convolution.
\begin{theorem}[Directional one-large-summand principle]\label{fce:lg:thm:convolution}
For each fixed $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:convlimit}
 \frac{(b^{*m})_{\boldsymbol k_N}}{b_{\boldsymbol k_N}}\longrightarrow m\Bmass^{m-1}.
\end{equation}
Uniformly in $m\ge1$ and all sufficiently large admissible $N$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:convbound}
 \frac{(b^{*m})_{\boldsymbol k_N}}{b_{\boldsymbol k_N}}
 \le C_0m^{\varsigma+1}\Bmass^{m-1}.
\end{equation}
Both assertions hold for a target with a fixed admissible shift.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In a decomposition
$\boldsymbol k_N=\boldsymbol k^{(1)}+\cdots+\boldsymbol k^{(m)}$, some
component has length at least $N/m$. Designate one such component. By
Lemma~\ref{fce:lg:lem:bounds} its weight is at most $C(N/m)^{-\varsigma}$.
Summing the other $m-1$ components freely contributes at most
$\Bmass^{m-1}$, and there are at most $m$ choices for the designated index.
The lower bound $b_{\boldsymbol k_N}\ge cN^{-\varsigma}$ proves
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:convbound}. If $m>N$ the convolution vanishes, so no
small-length exception is needed.

Now fix $m$ and a cutoff $H$. Consider decompositions in which all but one
component have length at most $H$. For $N>mH$ the large component is
unique. The fixed-shift ratio \eqref{fce:lg:eq:shift} gives, after division
by $b_{\boldsymbol k_N}$, the limit
\[
 m\left(\sum_{|\boldsymbol k|\le H}b_{\boldsymbol k}\right)^{m-1}.
\]
Every omitted decomposition has at least two components of length greater
than $H$. Designate a largest one and one other such component. The same
bound as before shows that their total normalized contribution is at most
\[
 C_m \Bmass^{m-2}\sum_{|\boldsymbol k|>H}b_{\boldsymbol k}
\]
for $m\ge2$. This tends to zero as $H\to\infty$. Taking first
$N\to\infty$ and then $H\to\infty$ proves \eqref{fce:lg:eq:convlimit};
$m=1$ is immediate. The proof uses only finite total mass, not finite first
moment. The shifted version follows from the shifted part of
Lemma~\ref{fce:lg:lem:bounds}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Entire functions of the bridge series]\label{fce:lg:cor:entire}
For any fixed $z\in\C$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:entire}
 \frac{[\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k_N}]\exp(z\Lcal(\boldsymbol p\boldsymbol x))}{b_{\boldsymbol k_N}}
 \longrightarrow z e^{z\Bmass}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 [\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k_N}]\E(\boldsymbol p\boldsymbol x)\sim e^{\Bmass} b_{\boldsymbol k_N}.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $N>0$, expand the exponential and use \eqref{fce:lg:eq:convlimit}
termwise. The absolute bounds
\[
 C_0m^{\varsigma+1}\Bmass^{m-1}|z|^m/m!
\]
are summable, so dominated convergence applies, also for negative or
complex $z$. The limiting series is $ze^{z\Bmass}$. Tonelli's theorem
applied to the nonnegative coefficient expansion also gives
$\E(\boldsymbol p)=e^{\Bmass}<\infty$.
\end{proof}

\section{The general balanced-composition formula}\label{fce:lg:sec:general}
\begin{theorem}[Arbitrary rational balanced compositions]\label{fce:lg:thm:general}
Under \eqref{fce:lg:eq:p}, let $\kappa(\boldsymbol p)=\E(\boldsymbol p)=e^{\Bmass}$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:general}
 e_{N\boldsymbol p}\sim \frac{\kappa(\boldsymbol p)}{N}\binom{N}{Np_1,\ldots,Np_q}
\end{equation}
as $N\to\infty$ through admissible multiples. The constant
$\kappa(\boldsymbol p)$ is finite, positive, and algebraic.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Corollary~\ref{fce:lg:cor:entire} states
$e_{N\boldsymbol p}\boldsymbol p^{N\boldsymbol p}
\sim \kappa(\boldsymbol p)b^0_{N\boldsymbol p}\boldsymbol p^{N\boldsymbol p}$.
Cancel the common weight. Finiteness has already been proved. Algebraicity
follows from the small-root formula at the rational critical weights,
taking the finite limit from $t<1$; equivalently take the product of its
limiting algebraic roots. In particular, this argument does not need to
infer algebraicity merely from convergence of a transcendental-looking
exponential series.
\end{proof}
For $p_i=1/r$, $N=rn$, and the steps \eqref{fce:eq:steps},
Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general} proves \eqref{fce:eq:probability}: this is
source~42's proof of the leading term of Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main}, which
uses no saddle point. Stirling gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:multstirling}
 \frac{(rn)!}{(n!)^r}
 =\frac{\sqrt r\,r^{rn}}{(2\pi n)^{(r-1)/2}}
 \left(1+\frac{r^{-1}-r}{12n}+O_r(n^{-2})\right),
\end{equation}
which proves the formula for $c(r)$ in \eqref{fce:eq:constant} and hence
the OEIS growth conjecture \eqref{fce:eq:oeisconj}.

Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general} and Corollary~\ref{fce:cor:rays} meet on
the step set \eqref{fce:eq:steps} with centred rational probabilities,
where they give the same leading term. Beyond that they are complementary:
Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general} covers every finite labelled list of
integer steps with both signs and needs only the finite bridge mass, but
gives the leading term only; Corollary~\ref{fce:cor:rays} adds all orders
and D-finiteness on its rays, for the rows' step set.

\begin{remark}[A probabilistic interpretation]\label{fce:lg:rem:probability}
Among all balanced words, the probability of satisfying the prefix
constraint is asymptotic to $\kappa_r/(rn)$. Thus $\kappa_r$ measures the
lattice correction to the naive inverse-length ballot probability. For
$r=2$ the exact probability is $1/(n+1)$; for $r=3$ it is also $1/(n+1)$.
The values $\kappa_2=2$ and $\kappa_3=3$ are consistent with both facts.
There is no assertion that the analogous probability is exactly a rational
function of $n$ for larger $r$.
\end{remark}

\section{A second route to the complete expansion: lattice coordinates}\label{fce:lg:sec:allorders}
Source~42 proves the all-orders part of Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} by the
same strategy as Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle} (Fourier extraction over the
composition torus, a moving square-root singularity, coefficient transfer
and Wick contraction), but in coordinates on the lattice of balanced
compositions rather than marker coordinates, and with an explicit
coefficient algorithm. Its formulation is printed here because
Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:first} builds on it. Like
Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}, it uses local complex analysis, and the proof
also specifies a finite coefficient procedure at every order.

\subsection{Composition coordinates and the unique Fourier saddle}
Fix $r$. Let
\[
 \Lambda=\{v\in\Z^r:\one\cdot v=\boldsymbol s\cdot v=0\},\qquad d=r-2.
\]
Choose an integral basis of this lattice, with basis matrix
$\mathsf V\in\Z^{r\times d}$, and put
$\Xi=(\mathsf V^{\mathsf T}\mathsf V)^{-1}\mathsf V^{\mathsf T}$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Cproperties}
 \Xi\mathsf V=I_d,\qquad \Xi\one=\Xi\boldsymbol s=0.
\end{equation}
Write $\xi_i\in\Q^d$ for the $i$th column of $\Xi$. At length $N=rn$, a
zero-sum composition is uniquely $n\one+\mathsf V\ell$ with $\ell\in\Z^d$,
and $\Xi\boldsymbol k=\ell$. For $z\in\C^d$ set
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:markedP}
 P_z(u)=\frac1r\sum_{i=1}^r e^{\xi_i\cdot z}u^{s_i},\qquad
 \widehat\E(t,z)=\E\!\left(\frac{t}{r}e^{\xi_1\cdot z},\ldots,
                         \frac{t}{r}e^{\xi_r\cdot z}\right).
\end{equation}
Fourier inversion, at precisely these lengths, gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Fourier}
 r^{-N}A_r(n)=\frac1{(2\pi)^d}\int_{[-\pi,\pi]^d}
 [t^N]\widehat\E(t,i\theta)\,d\theta.
\end{equation}
For $d=0$ the integral is absent.

The only saddle on this torus is zero, modulo its identified boundary. To
see the needed strictness, suppose for some $\theta,\phi$ that
$|P_{i\theta}(e^{i\phi})|=1$. Equality in the triangle inequality implies
$\xi_i\cdot\theta+s_i\phi\equiv\gamma\pmod{2\pi}$ for all $i$. Pair this
relation with any $v\in\Lambda$. It gives $\theta\cdot\Xi v\equiv0$, so
$\theta\in2\pi\Z^d$. Hence outside a neighborhood of zero the maximum over
$\phi$ is bounded strictly below one. The constant-term logarithm in
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:exp} then converges uniformly for $|t|<1+\epsilon$, giving
an exponentially small contribution to \eqref{fce:lg:eq:Fourier} there.

\subsection{The critical point and the square-root expansion}
There is a unique analytic function $\tau(z)$ near zero, with $\tau(0)=1$,
satisfying
\[
 \left.u\frac{\partial P_z}{\partial u}\right|_{u=\tau(z)}=0.
\]
This follows from the implicit-function theorem since
\[
 V=\frac1r\sum_i s_i^2>0.
\]
Put $P_*(z)=P_z(\tau(z))$ and $\Psi(z)=\log P_*(z)$. The identities
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:Cproperties} imply
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:phase}
 P_*(0)=1,\qquad \Psi'(0)=0,\qquad
 Q:=\Psi''(0)=\frac1r\Xi\Xi^{\mathsf T}>0.
\end{equation}
The notation $Q$ in this Part is a positive matrix, not the kernel
polynomial $Q_r(u)$, and not the Hessian $H$ of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}, which is the corresponding matrix in marker
coordinates.

At $t=P_*(z)^{-1}$, one small and one large root coalesce at
$u=\tau(z)$. The second derivative in $u$ is nonzero, so the analytic Morse
lemma, or direct coefficient solving, gives a convergent Puiseux expansion
in $(1-tP_*(z))^{1/2}$. The product of the other small roots is analytic in
$t,z$ in this neighborhood. This remains true even if individual
nonprincipal roots have an internal collision: the product of the roots
inside a separating contour is analytic by symmetric root functions. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:puiseux}
 \widehat\E(t,z)=\Upsilon_{\mathrm{an}}(t,z)
       +\sum_{k\ge0}\Upsilon_k(z)(1-tP_*(z))^{k+1/2},
\end{equation}
locally, with analytic coefficients. Its leading coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:H0}
 \Upsilon_0(z)=-\kappa_*(z)\sqrt{\frac{2P_*(z)}{\tau(z)^2 P_z''(\tau(z))}},
 \qquad \kappa_*(z)=\widehat\E(P_*(z)^{-1},z).
\end{equation}
Here $\kappa_*(z)$ denotes the critical value on the continued small-root
branch; it is not an assertion of absolute power-series convergence at
every complex marked point. In particular $\Upsilon_0(0)=-\kappa_r\sqrt2/\sqrt V$.

The time period is $\nu=1$ for odd $r$ and $\nu=2$ for even $r$. Indeed,
odd $r$ admits a zero step, while for even $r$ all steps are odd and a
two-step return is possible. At zero markings the only dominant
singularities are the corresponding time rotations of one. This follows as
well from the triangle-equality argument in the preceding subsection. At
nearby complex markings their local contributions are the same on
admissible lengths. Their separation from the remaining singularities
persists uniformly in a small neighborhood. Therefore coefficient transfer
from \eqref{fce:lg:eq:puiseux} yields, for $N=rn$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:univtransfer}
 [t^N]\widehat\E(t,z)=\nu P_*(z)^N N^{-3/2}
 \left(\sum_{j=0}^{M}\frac{\mathcal G_j(z)}{N^j}+O(N^{-M-1})\right),
\end{equation}
uniformly locally in $z$, with
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:G0}
 \mathcal G_0(z)=\frac{\kappa_*(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
 \sqrt{\frac{P_*(z)}{\tau(z)^2 P_z''(\tau(z))}}.
\end{equation}
Uniformity can be obtained by the same separated root contours and a
common slit neighborhood for the coalescing pair. Outside those
neighborhoods a coefficient contour can be pushed a fixed positive distance
beyond the dominant radius. The analytic local terms have no singular
contribution; the half-integral terms transfer by the gamma-ratio formula
in the next subsection. These observations give the uniform remainder in
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:univtransfer}, rather than merely a pointwise formal
expansion.

\subsection{An all-orders coefficient algorithm}
For nonintegral $\gamma$ define rational polynomials $t_i(\gamma)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:transfer}
 [t^N](1-t)^\gamma=
 \frac{N^{-\gamma-1}}{\Gamma(-\gamma)}
 \left(\sum_{i=0}^{M}\frac{t_i(\gamma)}{N^i}+O(N^{-M-1})\right).
\end{equation}
They are computed by expanding $\Gamma(N-\gamma)/\Gamma(N+1)$, as in
\eqref{fce:eq:transfer}; in particular $t_0=1$ and
$t_1(\gamma)=\gamma(\gamma+1)/2$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Gj}
 \mathcal G_j(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{j}
 \frac{\Upsilon_k(z)}{\Gamma(-k-1/2)}t_{j-k}(k+1/2).
\end{equation}
Write $\Psi(z)=\frac12z^{\mathsf T}Qz+\sum_{m\ge3}\Psi_m(z)$, where
$\Psi_m$ is homogeneous of degree $m$. Let $X$ be a centred
$d$-dimensional Gaussian with covariance $Q^{-1}$. Form the formal series
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:wickseries}
 \mathscr A(\hbar,X)=
 \left(\sum_{j\ge0}\hbar^{2j}\mathcal G_j(i\hbar X)\right)
 \exp\!\left(\sum_{m\ge3}\hbar^{m-2}\Psi_m(iX)\right).
\end{equation}
Every coefficient needed below is a finite polynomial in $X$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:betas}
 \chi_j=\Exp\,[\hbar^{2j}]\mathscr A(\hbar,X),\qquad
 \alpha_{r,j}=\frac{\chi_j}{r^j\mathcal G_0(0)}.
\end{equation}
Gaussian moments are computed exactly by pairings: an odd moment is zero,
and an even moment is the sum, over pairings of its factors, of products of
covariance entries. Thus \eqref{fce:lg:eq:betas} is a finite procedure, not
an appeal to fitted coefficients.

\begin{proof}[Second proof of the all-orders part of Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} (source~42)]
Insert \eqref{fce:lg:eq:univtransfer} into \eqref{fce:lg:eq:Fourier} and
scale $\theta=X/\sqrt N$ in a neighborhood of the unique saddle. By
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:phase},
\[
 N\Psi(iX/\sqrt N)
 =-\tfrac12X^{\mathsf T}QX+
 \sum_{m\ge3}N^{-(m-2)/2}\Psi_m(iX).
\]
Taylor expansion of the analytic amplitudes gives exactly
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:wickseries} with $\hbar=N^{-1/2}$. Terms of odd degree in
$\hbar$ integrate to zero: in each such term the total degree in $X$ is
odd. The resulting expansion is
\[
 r^{-N}A_r(n)=
 \frac{\nu N^{-(d+3)/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}\sqrt{\det Q}}
 \left(\sum_{j=0}^M\chi_jN^{-j}+O(N^{-M-1})\right).
\]
To justify truncation, first restrict to $|X|\le N^{\varepsilon_1}$ with
sufficiently small fixed $\varepsilon_1>0$. Analytic Taylor remainders there
are bounded by a chosen power of $N^{-1/2}$ times a polynomial in $|X|$ and
a Gaussian envelope. Expand to enough extra orders to reach the prescribed
$M$, and integrate that envelope. The part with $N^{\varepsilon_1}<|X|$
inside the fixed saddle neighborhood is exponentially small in
$N^{2\varepsilon_1}$, since $\Rea\Psi(i\theta)\le-c|\theta|^2$. The
complement of the saddle neighborhood was already exponentially
controlled. This proves the stated remainder at every order.

The leading coefficient agrees with \eqref{fce:eq:probability}, already
proved independently (Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general}); this also checks
all lattice and time-period factors. Since $N=rn$ and $d+3=r+1$,
normalizing by the leading coefficient gives \eqref{fce:eq:full} and
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:betas}.

Finally, all local Taylor and Puiseux coefficients at zero are obtained by
algebraic operations at algebraic roots of rational polynomials. Gamma
values at the negative half-integers contribute only a common $1/\sqrt\pi$
times rational factors. The covariance is rational. Consequently
$\chi_j/\mathcal G_0(0)$, and hence every $\alpha_{r,j}$, is algebraic.
The finite steps just described establish effectiveness.
\end{proof}
The scope of ``complete'' is the same as in the remark at the end of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}.

\section{First corrections by Gaussian moments}\label{fce:lg:sec:first}
\subsection{A coordinate-invariant first-correction formula}
The local calculation can use any rational full-rank $d\times r$ matrix
$\Xi$ annihilating $\one$ and $\boldsymbol s$, because an invertible
linear change of local coordinates cancels from the normalized
coefficients. In particular, consecutive second-difference rows $(1,-2,1)$
are convenient. They need not be the globally normalized Fourier
coordinates of Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:allorders}; only the local ratios
are being computed.

Put $L_i(x)=\xi_i\cdot x$ and define
\[
 \mathcal M_j(x)=\frac1r\sum_iL_i(x)^j,\qquad
 \mathcal M_{12}(x)=\frac1r\sum_i s_iL_i(x)^2.
\]
The critical phase has cubic and quartic terms
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:l34}
 \Psi_3(x)=\frac{\mathcal M_3(x)}6,\qquad
 \Psi_4(x)=\frac{\mathcal M_4(x)-3\mathcal M_2(x)^2}{24}
              -\frac{\mathcal M_{12}(x)^2}{8V}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, writing $v=\log\tau(z)$, the critical equation for
$\log(r^{-1}\sum_i e^{\xi_i\cdot z+s_iv})$ gives
$v=-\mathcal M_{12}(z)/(2V)+O(|z|^3)$. Substitution into its cumulant
expansion proves \eqref{fce:lg:eq:l34}.

Let $\mathfrak a(z)=\mathcal G_0(z)/\mathcal G_0(0)$ and write
$\mathfrak a'=\nabla\mathfrak a(0)$ and
$\mathfrak a''=\nabla^2\mathfrak a(0)$. Equations
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:wickseries}--\eqref{fce:lg:eq:betas} give
\begin{align}\label{fce:lg:eq:d1}
 r\,\alpha_{r,1}={}&\frac{\mathcal G_1(0)}{\mathcal G_0(0)}
 -\frac12\tr(Q^{-1}\mathfrak a'')\notag\\
 &+\Exp\left[\frac{(\mathfrak a'\cdot X)\mathcal M_3(X)}6
             +\Psi_4(X)-\frac{\mathcal M_3(X)^2}{72}\right].
\end{align}
The negative sign in the final term comes from $i^6=-1$. Omitting it
changes the answer.

\subsection{Computing the local kernel terms}
Here are explicit formulas sufficient to reproduce the first correction. At
zero markings put $P=P_0$ and expand the principal small root at $t=1-w^2$
as $u=1+a_1w+a_2w^2+a_3w^3+O(w^4)$, as in Appendix~\ref{fce:app:algorithm}.
Solving $P(u)=1/(1-w^2)$ gives
\begin{align}\label{fce:lg:eq:v123}
 a_1&=-\sqrt{2/P''(1)},\qquad
 a_2=-\frac{P'''(1)a_1^2}{6P''(1)},\notag\\
 a_3&=\frac{1-\frac12P''(1)a_2^2
                -\frac12P'''(1)a_1^2a_2
                -\frac1{24}P''''(1)a_1^4}{P''(1)a_1}.
\end{align}
When the nonprincipal interior roots $\omega\in\mathcal R_r$ are simple,
where $\mathcal R_r$ is the multiset of roots of $Q_r(u)/(u-1)^2$ in
$|u|<1$, their $w^2$ coefficients are $1/P'(\omega)$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:g1ratio}
 \frac{\mathcal G_1(0)}{\mathcal G_0(0)}=\frac38-\frac32
 \left(\frac{a_3}{a_1}+1+\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal R_r}
                    \frac1{\omega P'(\omega)}\right).
\end{equation}
For nonsimple nonprincipal roots, compute the derivative of their analytic
product instead of differentiating the individual roots; the general
algorithm in Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:allorders} is unaffected. The roots
for $r=3,5$ used below are simple (or absent).

For the amplitude derivatives, use
\[
 \log\mathcal G_0(z)=\log\kappa_*(z)-\tfrac12\log V(z)-\tfrac12\log(2\pi),
 \qquad V(z)=\left.\frac{\partial^2}{\partial v^2}
 \log P_z(e^v)\right|_{v=\log\tau(z)}.
\]
The root product gives
\[
 \log\kappa_*(z)=\log P_*(z)+\log\tau(z)
       +\sum_\omega\log\omega(z)-\xi_-\cdot z+\text{constant}.
\]
Here $\xi_-$ is the column for the minimum step. The derivatives of a
simple nonprincipal root follow by implicit differentiation of
$P_z(\omega(z))=P_*(z)$:
\begin{align}\label{fce:lg:eq:rootderivatives}
 \omega_j&=-P_{z_j}(\omega)/P'(\omega),\notag\\
 (\log\omega)_{jk}
 &=\frac{Q_{jk}-P_{z_jz_k}(\omega)
      -P_{uz_j}(\omega)\omega_k-P_{uz_k}(\omega)\omega_j
      -P''(\omega)\omega_j\omega_k}{\omega P'(\omega)}
      -\frac{\omega_j\omega_k}{\omega^2}.
\end{align}
All quantities on the right are evaluated at zero markings. In addition,
\[
 (\log\tau)_{jk}=-\frac1{rV}\sum_i s_i\xi_{i,j}\xi_{i,k},\quad
 V_j=\frac1r\sum_i s_i^2\xi_{i,j},\quad
 V_{jk}=\frac1r\sum_i s_i^2\xi_{i,j}\xi_{i,k}-VQ_{jk}.
\]
The last identity uses the symmetry $\sum_i s_i^3=0$. These formulas make
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:d1} an exact algebraic calculation.

\subsection{Three evaluated cases}
The two exact rows of Section~\ref{fce:sec:constants} give the second-order
terms
\begin{align}\label{fce:lg:eq:r23corr}
 A_2(n)&=\frac{4^n}{\sqrt\pi n^{3/2}}
       \left(1-\frac9{8n}+\frac{145}{128n^2}+O(n^{-3})\right),\notag\\
 A_3(n)&=\frac{\sqrt3\,27^n}{2\pi n^2}
       \left(1-\frac{11}{9n}+\frac{101}{81n^2}+O(n^{-3})\right).
\end{align}
For $r=5$, formulas \eqref{fce:lg:eq:d1}--\eqref{fce:lg:eq:rootderivatives}
give the value of $\alpha_{5,1}$ in \eqref{fce:eq:fivecorrection},
\[
 \alpha_{5,1}=-\frac{13}{10}+\frac{13\sqrt5}{50},
\]
by a contraction different from the one of Section~\ref{fce:sec:correction}.
In the multinomial normalization this is
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:r5ratio}
 \frac{A_5(n)}{\kappa_5(5n)!/(5n(n!)^5)}
 =1+\frac{-9/10+13\sqrt5/50}{n}+O(n^{-2}),
\end{equation}
that is, $\Delta_5/5$ in \eqref{fce:eq:ratiofirst}. The shipped
\path{code/42-late-growing-first_correction.py} carries out the rational
Gaussian moments and algebraic root differentiation exactly for $r=3,5$.
It obtains
\[
 \alpha_{3,1}=-11/9,\qquad \alpha_{5,1}=-13/10+13\sqrt5/50
                      =-0.71862232585005467893\ldots.
\]
The value of $\alpha_{3,1}$ is obtained here from the general formula and
agrees with the value read off the closed form in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:constants}. These values are not estimates from
sequence data and do not use the conjectured recurrence.

\section{Primitive excursions and the giant component}\label{fce:lg:sec:components}
An excursion is \emph{primitive} if it is nonempty and has no zero-height
prefix before its endpoint; primitive excursions are the arches of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:contacts}. A one-step zero excursion is primitive.
Every excursion factors uniquely at its returns to height zero. Let
$\mathcal I(\boldsymbol x)$ enumerate primitive excursions; then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:I}
 \E=\frac1{1-\mathcal I},\qquad \mathcal I=1-e^{-\Lcal}.
\end{equation}
We retain the general critical composition \eqref{fce:lg:eq:p} in the first
theorem and put $\kappa=\E(\boldsymbol p)$.

\begin{lemma}[Primitive weights]\label{fce:lg:lem:primitive}
If $i_{\boldsymbol k}=[\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k}]\mathcal I(\boldsymbol p\boldsymbol x)$,
then
\[
 0\le i_{\boldsymbol k}\le b_{\boldsymbol k},\qquad
 \sum_{\boldsymbol k} i_{\boldsymbol k}=1-\kappa^{-1},\qquad
 i_{\boldsymbol k_N}\sim \kappa^{-1}b_{\boldsymbol k_N}.
\]
The last assertion holds also after a fixed admissible shift.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A primitive excursion of length $m>1$ has a unique minimum among its cyclic
vertices before the endpoint. Its $m$ rotations are distinct: a nontrivial
word period would give an earlier zero return. No rotation class can
contain two primitive excursions. Consequently $m$ times the number of
primitive words of a fixed composition is at most the number of all bridge
words of that composition, proving $i_{\boldsymbol k}\le b_{\boldsymbol k}$.
Length one is immediate. The mass identity follows from
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:I}. Finally apply Corollary~\ref{fce:lg:cor:entire} with
$z=-1$ and use $\mathcal I=1-e^{-\Lcal}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A size-biased geometric law]\label{fce:lg:thm:K}
Choose uniformly an excursion of composition $N\boldsymbol p$, and let $J_N$
be its number of primitive factors. For each fixed $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Klaw}
 \Prob(J_N=k)\longrightarrow
 k\kappa^{-2}(1-\kappa^{-1})^{k-1}.
\end{equation}
All fixed moments converge. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Kmoments}
 \Exp J_N\longrightarrow2\kappa-1,\qquad
 \Prob(J_N=1)\longrightarrow \kappa^{-2}.
\end{equation}
For the OEIS rows take $\kappa=\kappa_r$ and $N=rn$.
\end{theorem}
For the rows this is the law of Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:contacts}, proved
there by the analytic transfer of Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}: $J_N$ is
the number $J_n$ of arches, $C_n=J_n-1$, and $2\kappa_r-1=1+2(\kappa_r-1)$.
The proof below is source~42's second route, by the cutoff argument of
Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:convolution}; it also covers every centred
rational composition and every fixed moment.
\begin{proof}
Repeat the cutoff proof of Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:convolution} for $i$ in
place of $b$. Lemma~\ref{fce:lg:lem:primitive} supplies the same global
bound and the local ratio
$i_{\boldsymbol k_N-\boldsymbol\ell}/b_{\boldsymbol k_N}\to \kappa^{-1}$.
With $\eta=1-\kappa^{-1}$, it follows that
\[
 (i^{*k})_{\boldsymbol k_N}/b_{\boldsymbol k_N}\longrightarrow k\kappa^{-1}\eta^{k-1}.
\]
Divide by the total excursion coefficient, asymptotic to
$\kappa b_{\boldsymbol k_N}$. A uniform bound of the form
$Ck^{\varsigma+1}\eta^{k-1}$ follows by designating a largest component.
Since $\eta<1$, it proves convergence of all fixed moments by dominated
summation. The limiting probability generating function is
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Kpgf}
 \frac{v}{\kappa^2(1-\eta v)^2}.
\end{equation}
Differentiating at $v=1$ proves the mean formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[One giant primitive component]\label{fce:lg:thm:giant}
Write a uniform excursion of composition $N\boldsymbol p$ as
$\mathcal U_N \Omega_N \mathcal V_N$, where $\Omega_N$ is a longest primitive
component and ties are resolved by choosing the first one. Then
$(\mathcal U_N,\mathcal V_N)$ converges in total variation to a pair
$(\mathcal U,\mathcal V)$ of independent finite excursions with
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:boltzmann}
 \Prob(\mathcal U=u)=\frac{\boldsymbol p^{\operatorname{comp}(u)}}{\kappa},\qquad
 \Prob(\mathcal V=v)=\frac{\boldsymbol p^{\operatorname{comp}(v)}}{\kappa}.
\end{equation}
In particular $N-|\Omega_N|=O_{\Prob}(1)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix two concrete finite excursions $u,v$ and put
$\boldsymbol\ell=\operatorname{comp}(u)+\operatorname{comp}(v)$. For all
sufficiently large $N$, an excursion of the form $u j v$ with $j$ primitive
of composition $N\boldsymbol p-\boldsymbol\ell$ automatically has $j$ as its
unique longest factor. Its probability is the ratio of that primitive count
to $e_{N\boldsymbol p}$. The shifted primitive asymptotic gives the limit
$\boldsymbol p^{\boldsymbol\ell}/\kappa^2$, exactly the product in
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:boltzmann}.

The sum of these limiting probabilities over all ordered pairs of finite
excursions is $\E(\boldsymbol p)^2/\kappa^2=1$. Pointwise convergence on
this countable space to a distribution of total mass one implies
total-variation convergence: restrict first to a finite subset with
limiting mass at least $1-\epsilon$, then control its complement. Tightness
of the two finite decoration lengths follows. It also shows that the
probability of competing macroscopic factors tends to zero.
\end{proof}
This limit is compatible with a nontrivial number of small factors: a
giant \emph{primitive} component need not mean that the whole excursion is
primitive.

\subsection{A tight gap with an infinite-mean limit}
For the uniform alphabet, let $D_{\mathrm{dec}}=|\mathcal U|+|\mathcal V|$
be the limiting decoration length. Its probability generating function is
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Dpgf}
 \Exp t^{D_{\mathrm{dec}}}=\left(\frac{\E(t/r,\ldots,t/r)}{\kappa_r}\right)^2.
\end{equation}
The square-root expansion at $t=1$ gives
\[
 \Exp t^{D_{\mathrm{dec}}}=1-\frac{2\sqrt2}{\sqrt V}\sqrt{1-t}+O(1-t).
\]
Transfer and summation along the permitted time lattice yield
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:Dtail}
 \Prob(D_{\mathrm{dec}}\ge m)\sim\frac{2\sqrt2}{\sqrt{V\pi}}\,m^{-1/2},\qquad
 \Exp D_{\mathrm{dec}}=\infty.
\end{equation}
For even $r$, the support is even; the factor two in coefficient transfer
cancels the half-density when the tail is summed. Thus
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:Dtail} holds as written for unrestricted integer
$m\to\infty$. Tightness of $N-|\Omega_N|$ does not imply convergence of its
expectation to a finite constant. This is a useful warning against an
otherwise tempting interpretation of the giant-component theorem.

\section{Ratios, curvature and an explicit rounding bound}\label{fce:lg:sec:inverse}
\subsection{Ratios and eventual strict log-convexity}
With $\beta=(r+1)/2$ and $\lambda=r\log r$ as in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:inverse}, the complete expansion gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:ratio}
 \frac{A_r(n+1)}{A_r(n)}
 =r^r\left(1-\frac{\beta}{n}
 +\frac{\beta(\beta+1)/2-\alpha_{r,1}}{n^2}
 +O_r(n^{-3})\right).
\end{equation}
Taking sufficiently many terms before taking second differences gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:logconvex}
 \log A_r(n+1)-2\log A_r(n)+\log A_r(n-1)
 =\frac{\beta}{n^2}+O_r(n^{-3})>0
\end{equation}
for all sufficiently large $n$. Therefore every fixed row is eventually
strictly log-convex, and its successive ratios eventually increase to $r^r$
from below. This is not a claim of log-convexity at every finite index. A
remainder controlled only by $o(1)$ would not suffice to infer the
curvature; the higher-order expansion supplies the needed control.

\subsection{The inverse dominant sector}
Source~42 inverts the dominant sector exactly as
Section~\ref{fce:sec:inverse} does: the large real solution of
$Y=K_re^{\lambda x}x^{-\beta}$ is \eqref{fce:eq:lambertcore}, on the lower
real branch (the principal branch would select the small solution, not the
large inverse); the formula follows by raising the model equation to the
power $-1/\beta$ and applying the defining equation $We^W=z$. Its
correction terms are those of \eqref{fce:eq:inversefirst}, with
$b_1=\alpha_{r,1}$ and $b_2=\alpha_{r,2}-\frac12\alpha_{r,1}^2$, obtained
by successive formal substitution or Newton correction; at every order the
next coefficient is determined linearly, since the derivative of the
leading logarithm is $\lambda-\beta/x$, bounded away from zero for large
$x$. For orientation, writing $L_Y=\log Y$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:loginverse}
 x(Y)=\frac{L_Y+\beta\log L_Y-\log K_r-\beta\log\lambda}{\lambda}
 +O_r\left(\frac{\log L_Y}{L_Y}\right).
\end{equation}
For A215570 the first correction to the Lambert model is positive, because
$\alpha_{5,1}<0$; compare \eqref{fce:eq:inversefive}.

\subsection{Discrete thresholds and the rounding boundary}
Let $N_r(Y)=\min\{n:A_r(n)\ge Y\}$ for sufficiently large $Y$, as in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:thresholds}. The sequence is eventually strictly
increasing by \eqref{fce:lg:eq:ratio}, so this tail threshold is
unambiguous. (2026-10-05 note: for $r\ge3$ it is strictly increasing at every
index, Proposition~\ref{fce:dx:prop:supermult}, so $N_r(Y)$ is unambiguous for
every $Y$.) Let $x_M(Y)$ solve the logarithmic expansion truncated after
$x^{-M}$. There exists $\varpi_{r,M}$ such that, for all large $Y$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:rounding}
 \left\lceil x_M(Y)-\varpi_{r,M}(\log Y)^{-M-1}\right\rceil
 \le N_r(Y)\le
 \left\lceil x_M(Y)+\varpi_{r,M}(\log Y)^{-M-1}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
This follows by comparing the logarithmic error at integer arguments with
the derivative bounded away from zero. Away from the shrinking
neighborhoods of integers the two bounds coincide. Near an integer, the
error interval is necessary; a blanket claim that one may always take a
ceiling of a truncated transseries would be false.

\begin{remark}[Repository context]\label{fce:lg:rem:staircase}
Inequality \eqref{fce:lg:eq:rounding} is an instance of parts~(2)--(3) of
the staircase theorem \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} of the canonical
transseries volume (Section~\ref{fce:sec:repository}), with
$\eta=\varpi_{r,M}(\log Y)^{-M-1}$; its separation clause is formalized as
the Lean theorem \texttt{Fabius.staircase\_separation} and the failure of
separation for arbitrarily small $\eta$ as
\texttt{Fabius.staircase\_separation\_fails}. Those are general statements
about ceilings; they do not verify the asymptotic inputs of this report,
and no novelty is claimed for the rounding statement.
\end{remark}

\section{Source 42's computations}\label{fce:lg:sec:checks}
\subsection{Exact dynamic programming}
For an arbitrary composition $\boldsymbol k$, let
$\mathrm{ht}(\boldsymbol k)=\boldsymbol s\cdot\boldsymbol k$. Excursion-prefix
counts satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{fce:lg:eq:dp}
 F(\boldsymbol0)=1,\qquad
 F(\boldsymbol k)=\begin{cases}
 0,&\mathrm{ht}(\boldsymbol k)<0,\\
 \displaystyle\sum_{i:k_i>0}F(\boldsymbol k-\boldsymbol e_i),&\mathrm{ht}(\boldsymbol k)\ge0.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
The desired value is $F(n\one)$; this is the recurrence of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:checks}, implemented independently. This recursion is
exact: every admissible word is obtained uniquely by deleting its last
letter. The implementation traverses a rectangular composition box in
lexicographic order, which is topological for coordinate decrements. It
uses integer arithmetic and no row recurrence conjecture.

Two companion arrays count primitive words and the sum of the numbers of
returns. For the primitive array, do not propagate any nonempty
zero-height state. For the moment array, add the current count whenever a
newly reached state has height zero. This gives independent finite tests of
both \eqref{fce:lg:eq:Klaw} and \eqref{fce:lg:eq:Kmoments}.

\subsection{Independent coefficient and algebra checks}
Source~42's program performs the following finite checks.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item The first seven $r=4,5$ values are checked against the displayed OEIS
data. Full independently computed ranges are $0\le n\le30$ for $r=4$,
$0\le n\le15$ for $r=5$, $0\le n\le9$ for $r=6$, and $0\le n\le5$ for $r=7$.
\item The bridge--excursion identity is checked coefficientwise in small
multivariate boxes using the independent Euler identity \eqref{fce:eq:expcheck}.
The tested boxes use $(r,\text{bound})=(2,5),(3,4),(4,3),(5,2)$.
\item The sextic elimination identity is verified as an exact
rational-function identity, including its specified branch at the origin.
\item Kernel polynomials are deflated exactly by $(u-1)^2$. Their interior
roots are evaluated at high precision for $2\le r\le12$ and the four
radical constants are cross-checked. These decimal evaluations are not
interval-certified root isolations.
\item The first-correction formula is evaluated symbolically for $r=3,5$
using exact Gaussian moments. The $r=3$ result is independently checked
against its elementary exact formula.
\end{enumerate}
The shipped \path{data/42-late-growing-verification.txt} and
\path{data/42-late-growing-first_correction.txt} record the successful
runs. The CSV files retain exact counts and decimal diagnostics. All the
supplied assertions passed in the recorded run. The full dynamic program
stores arrays of size $(n+1)^r$, so memory, rather than asymptotic
arithmetic sophistication, is the main practical limitation of this
deliberately simple independent verifier.

\subsection{Claims that remain outside the certificate}
The proofs are mathematical arguments in this report, not Lean or Rocq
proofs. The scripts certify their stated finite computations, not every
analytic estimate. No numerical fit is used to prove an asymptotic
coefficient. No low-order recurrence is used as an assumption. No actual
recurrence operator for $r=4$ is supplied. No claim of exhaustive
literature priority, uniformity in $r$, exponentially improved asymptotics,
or an explicit finite log-convexity threshold is made.

\part{Research directions and conclusion}\label{fce:part:three}
\section{Further research questions and topics}\label{fce:sec:future}
The research directions of the two sources are merged here: each subsection
states source~61's question and then what source~42 adds; two subsections
are source~42's alone. The eight questions of source~75-03 (Part~IV) were
added on October~2, 2026, as sentences marked with its name. The
A215570 question of sources~61 and~42 is answered by source~75-03, and
source~75-03's question on all orders for every row by
Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main}. (2026-10-02 note: the four questions of
source~77-44, Part~\ref{fce:ba:part}, coincide with subsections below; they
are listed in Section~\ref{fce:ba:sec:checks}.) (2026-10-05 note: the questions of sources~101-90 and~101-86, Part~\ref{fce:dx:part},
coincide with subsections below and are credited there;
Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions} lists them as items 101.1--101.6.)
\subsection{Certify the explicit A215570 recurrence (resolved)}
The sextic \eqref{fce:eq:sextic}, its specified formal branch, and a diagonal
representation give a concrete input for creative telescoping. The next
objective is to compute a certified annihilator and prove that the
order-three operator in Kauers--Koutschan Conjecture 15 is a right factor
annihilating the same solution, with all initial conditions and singular
indices checked. Merely matching additional terms would not accomplish
this. A useful outcome would be a compact certificate independently
checkable without a large symbolic-computation environment. Source~42
(its project~1) proposes the same route and asks for a machine-readable
telescoping certificate and an independent checker: resolving the named
conjecture, not just the existence of some recurrence, is the point.

\emph{Resolved (2026-10-02).} Source~75-03 proves the conjecture
(Theorem~\ref{fce:kk:thm:recurrence}) with exactly such a compact
certificate (Appendix~\ref{fce:kk:app:certificate}) and an exact checker,
for every $n\ge0$, so that no initial conditions or singular indices remain
to be checked. Its route differs from the one proposed here: it does not
eliminate from the sextic, but reduces the diagonal to the one-variable
coefficient \eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive} and telescopes its Legendre
residue representation. Minimality of the order-three operator is not
claimed. The recurrence questions for A215562 and the higher rows remain
open (next subsection).

\subsection{Find and explain the fourth-row recurrence}
A215562 is D-finite by the proof here, but its minimal recurrence and its
computational complexity remain to be determined. The even-step parity
and the larger kernel degree should be incorporated before elimination,
not left for a generic telescoper to rediscover. What upper bounds on
recurrence order and coefficient degree can be proved as functions of $r$?
Do the even and odd alphabets have qualitatively different minimal orders?
Source~42 (project~2) notes that the generic small-root subset polynomial
has degree at most $\binom63=20$ in this case, proposes exploiting the
reciprocal and label-reversal symmetries before telescoping, and asks for a
comparison of recurrence order, coefficient degree and bit complexity with
direct composition dynamic programming. Source~75-03 asks for the same
operator: eliminate the product of three small roots of the marked
degree-six kernel and compute a diagonal telescoper, ideally with a compact
rational certificate. The degree bound $20$ suggests a larger problem than
the five-symbol sextic but does not predict the minimal order; a smaller
auxiliary coefficient representation, analogous to
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive}, would be especially valuable. (2026-10-05 note: source~101-90 lists ``explicit minimal recurrences'' among its open
questions; its D-finiteness proof by an $r$-section,
\eqref{fce:dx:eq:rsection}, is again existence only; item~101.1 of
Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions}.)

\subsection{Automate all-order coefficient extraction}
The proof supplies finite algebraic operations, but a practical system
should exploit residual-marker coordinates, symmetry, and Wick
contractions to avoid dense high-dimensional tensors. An immediate target
is exact $\alpha_{r,1},\alpha_{r,2},\alpha_{r,3}$ for $r=4,6,7,8$, together
with machine-readable algebraic-number specifications. (2026-10-02 note:
source~77-44 supplies $\alpha_{4,1}$ exactly in $\Q(\varrho)$,
Theorem~\ref{fce:ba:thm:alpha41}; $\alpha_{4,2}$, $\alpha_{4,3}$ and the rows
$r=6,7,8$ remain open.) For $r=5$, the
field $\Q(\sqrt5)$ makes an especially clean regression suite.
Source~42 (project~3) proposes the same engine built on
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:Gj} and \eqref{fce:lg:eq:betas}, with exact algebraic
numbers, branch-isolation certificates, symmetry reduction and regression
tests from $r=2,3$, and targets $\alpha_{r,2},\alpha_{r,3}$ for $r=4,5,6$;
its first-correction program is a prototype, not this engine. For $r=5$
the target is met through $\alpha_{5,8}$ (Proposition~\ref{fce:kk:prop:alpha}),
by the recurrence rather than by such an engine. Source~75-03 asks whether
its transfer argument, which gives only a nonsharp $O(n^{-1/5})$ error, can
be refined to all orders for every fixed alphabet; Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main}
already does so, so its question reduces to the targets above (it names an
explicit, proved $1/n$ correction for $r=4,6,7$; 2026-10-02 note: for
$r=4$ it is Theorem~\ref{fce:ba:thm:alpha41}, for $r=6,7$ still open) and to a uniform version of
the convolution method giving a better general error without the whole
expansion. It also asks for the minimal polynomials and degrees of the
constants \eqref{fce:eq:finiteconstant} and their dependence on parity,
whether reciprocal symmetry reduces the generic subset-product degree
bound substantially, and for rational isolating boxes of the inner roots
that prove decimal intervals for $\kappa_r$. (2026-10-05 note: source~101-86
meets this target for $r=5$ at second order \emph{by the engine} rather than
by the recurrence: Theorem~\ref{fce:dx:thm:alpha52} computes $\alpha_{5,2}$
by exact Gaussian moments, in agreement with
Proposition~\ref{fce:kk:prop:alpha}. Source~101-90 states the rule for every
$r$ in marker coordinates, \eqref{fce:dx:eq:recipe}; evaluated at first order
it reproduces $\alpha_{4,1}$ and $\alpha_{5,1}$ numerically,
Remark~\ref{fce:dx:rem:recipecheck}. Its non-claim on the smallest field of
the coefficients is item~101.5 of Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions}, the
engine item~101.6. The values $\alpha_{4,2}$, $\alpha_{4,3}$ and the rows
$r=6,7,8$ remain open.)

\subsection{Determine sharp asymptotic error constants}
Turn the separated kernel contours and local Taylor bounds into explicit
numerical inequalities. This would provide certified approximation
intervals at finite $n$, certified threshold inversion near an integer
boundary, and explicit eventual-log-convexity thresholds (source~42,
project~4). Source~75-03 asks the same for the constant $C$ of its rounded
threshold for A215570 (Section~\ref{fce:kk:sec:inverse}): explicit
finite-$Y$ bounds that bracket exact threshold searches. (2026-10-05 note: sources~101-86 and~101-90 ask the same for their inverses, which assert
``no numerical error constant or unconditional exact-ceiling rule''; item~101.3
of Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions}.)

\subsection{Determine the exponentially small sectors}
The dominant expansion does not identify subdominant critical points,
connection constants, or Stokes phenomena. Once an explicit differential
or recurrence operator is available, compare its formal solutions with
complex kernel saddles. Which exponentially small contributions are
actually present in the combinatorial solution? Can optimal truncation
and rigorous exponentially improved error bounds be obtained without
first computing a minimal recurrence? Source~42 (project~9) asks the same:
identify non-dominant singularities of the algebraic kernel and the
relevant complex composition saddles, which of them contribute after
diagonal extraction, their exponential scales, and their Stokes
transitions under parameter changes. This is what would be needed to
replace the dominant-sector expansion by a more complete transseries.
For A215570 the question is now concrete (source~75-03): the proved
recurrence has normalized characteristic roots $1,1,2/27$
(Remark~\ref{fce:kk:rem:scope}). Determine the decomposition of the actual
solution into its dominant and exponentially smaller components, with
connection constants, and whether and how optimally truncated dominant
expansions detect the third mode. (2026-10-05 note: sources~101-86
and~101-90 list the smaller exponential sectors among their open questions;
item~101.2 of Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions}.)

\subsection{Let the alphabet grow}
Our estimates keep $r$ fixed. When $r=r(n)$ grows, kernel-root separation,
Gaussian dimension, and constants in the remainder estimates all change.
Determine useful uniform ranges, and analyze the large-$r$ behavior of
$\kappa_r$ separately along even and odd alphabets. This would connect the
fixed-row problem to the less regular column direction of A215561.
Source~42 (project~5) adds that the parity split is real: odd alphabets
include a zero step, while even alphabets have time period two, so
fixed-$r$ error constants cannot simply be reused. It asks for expansions
of $\log\kappa_r$ separately for even and odd $r$, and for a range of joint
growth $r=r(n)$ in which the fixed-row formula remains uniform.
Source~75-03 asks for the same simultaneous limit and, separately, for the
column $n=1$ of the array, which is A147681; none of the fixed-row theorems
settles that column problem. (2026-10-05 note: source~101-90 asks for ``uniform
regimes in which the alphabet size grows''; in its coordinates the
multiplicity $2h$ and $\det H_{\rm ex}=12(r-1)/(r^r(r+1))$ show the
$r$-dependence explicitly; item~101.4 of
Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:questions}.)

\subsection{Resolve the mean size of the non-giant remainder}
The limit gap $D_{\mathrm{dec}}$ has an $m^{-1/2}$ tail and infinite mean
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:Dtail}. Determine the growth of $\Exp(N-|\Omega_N|)$ and
higher moments under exact composition conditioning, including their
leading constants. Establish a transition law when the gap is a positive
fraction of $N$ and quantify the probability of two large components.
Weak convergence alone does not answer these questions (source~42,
project~6).

\subsection{Approach the boundary of the composition simplex}
In Corollary~\ref{fce:cor:rays}, positivity of all probabilities is essential
for the full-dimensional saddle. If some $p_i$ tend to zero, the determinant
in \eqref{fce:eq:detH} degenerates and the effective alphabet may change.
Find uniform transition formulas interpolating between adjacent alphabet
sizes and identify the scaling regimes in which a different asymptotic
power appears. Source~42 (project~8) also asks at which scale the
giant-component law of Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:giant} changes; its
Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general} likewise assumes that every $p_i$ is
strictly positive. Source~75-03 asks the same when some type frequencies
tend to zero with $N$: the multinomial covariance degenerates, the
effective number of types can change and the power $(r+1)/2$ need not stay
uniform; a useful theory would connect the full-dimensional regime to the
lower-dimensional faces with explicit crossover scales.

\subsection{Refine path statistics under exact composition}
The contact law is only one consequence of the marked kernel. Natural
next targets are its finite-$n$ corrections, the maximum height, the area
under the excursion, and functional convergence after diffusive scaling.
These require additional arguments: neither the count asymptotic nor the
contact law alone proves a Brownian-excursion limit or independence of
other statistics. Source~42 (project~7) asks for the functional limit
after diffusive height scaling jointly with the Boltzmann decorations of
Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:giant} and the primitive-factor count. Exact
composition is a stronger conditioning than an endpoint constraint, and a
satisfactory theorem should specify the covariance normalization and
decide whether the limiting giant shape is asymptotically independent of
the decorations. Source~75-03 asks for the same functional limit under
exact content, expecting a Brownian-excursion-type limit with a
content-determined variance, and names the height maximum, the area and
the locations of short primitive components as observables; these need
probability arguments beyond the scalar asymptotic.

\subsection{Formalize the exact-to-analytic interface}
A staged ProveIt development could first formalize the word--walk
bijection, the finite-state recurrences, the bridge coefficient identity,
and the formal algebraic kernel relation. A second stage would address
algebraic-series diagonal closure and critical-root selection. The most
substantial analytic stage is a reusable uniform marked square-root
transfer theorem with a nondegenerate composition saddle and explicit
lattice periods. Keeping these stages separate would prevent finite
computations from being mistaken for proofs of analytic hypotheses.
Source~42 (project~10) proposes to begin instead with its convolution
route: the exact bridge coefficients, the factorial ratio lemma, the
convolution cutoff bound and dominated exponential summation
(Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:convolution}) give the leading growth theorem with
a relatively small analytic interface, so that the substantial all-orders
complex analysis need not be a prerequisite for a formally verified
leading result. The kernel identity and the multivariate saddle estimates
can be later modules. Source~75-03 proposes a formalization of the
recurrence proof as a chain of small reusable certificates: content
vectors, prefix validity, reversal and zero-step insertion; the marked
kernel algebra and the Lagrange extraction; the coefficient identities
and the polynomial certificate in a rational-function ring; the Legendre
recurrence and derivative identities with the vanishing residue of a
rational derivative; and, separately, the Fourier bounds and the
composition-transfer theorem. These are distinct obligations: checking the
printed certificate alone does not establish the counting semantics.

\section{Conclusion}
The fixed-row problem is governed by a finite one-dimensional step set,
not by an intrinsically growing transfer matrix. Marking composition
before taking a diagonal makes this structure visible. The resulting
algebraic ambient series gives D-finiteness; its bridge logarithm gives
an exact reference coefficient; and a marked critical-point calculation
converts that coefficient into the conjectured row asymptotic with an
algebraic constant.

The same analysis yields a complete dominant inverse-power expansion,
a concrete correction for A215570, a contact distribution, and an
asymptotic inversion procedure. A separate arithmetic argument shows
that every row generating function above the Catalan case is
transcendental. These results settle the stated fixed-row growth
conjecture and the recurrence-existence issue while leaving the explicit
recurrence certificates and exponentially small sectors as sharply
specified next problems. (2026-10-02 note: Part~\ref{fce:kk:part} supplies
the certificate for A215570; the one for A215562 remains open.)

Source~42 reaches the leading formula by a different mechanism: along a
strictly positive balanced composition ray, the coefficients of an
exponential of the log-excursion series are dominated by one large
summand, and the remaining summands contribute exactly the critical
excursion value. The same mechanism, applied to the factorization into
primitive excursions, explains the limiting number of returns and the
single giant component with its heavy-tailed finite decorations.
Certifying minimal recurrences, obtaining effective finite error bounds,
and passing to growing alphabets are distinct next steps, not conclusions
hidden inside the present theorems.

Source~75-03, finally, finds for the five-symbol row an exact
representation much smaller than the content-state dynamic program: a
positive one-variable coefficient with a Legendre-polynomial
interpretation. It yields an explicit proof of the published order-three
recurrence and, with the first correction, every dominant asymptotic
coefficient and its inverse, while an explicit four-symbol recurrence,
uniform growing-alphabet limits and the exponentially small sectors stay
separate tasks.

Source~77-44 reaches the leading theorems a fourth time, by the same
kernel, saddle and mark-extraction route, and contributes the first
correction of the four-symbol row; the recurrence for that row is still
open.

Sources~101-90 and~101-86 reach the leading theorems a fifth and a sixth time
by direct marked-excursion extraction in two further coordinate systems,
which show that the number of dominant torus points belongs to the
coordinates, not to the problem. They add a recurrence-free second correction
for A215570, the constant of its zero-free count, and the supermultiplicativity
that makes every row with $r\ge3$ strictly increasing at every index.

\part{The Kauers--Koutschan recurrence and the five-symbol row}\label{fce:kk:part}

Part~IV was added on October~2, 2026, in batch~75 of ProveIt's
incoming-reports intake, from a third manuscript on the same problem
(source~75-03), after the closing Part~III. It proves what Parts~I and~II
left open and Part~III lists as an open question: the explicit
order-three recurrence for A215570 conjectured by Kauers and Koutschan
(Theorem~\ref{fce:kk:thm:recurrence}). With that recurrence it computes the
exact coefficients $\alpha_{5,2},\ldots,\alpha_{5,8}$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:full} for $r=5$ (Table~\ref{fce:kk:tab:alpha}). About two
thirds of source~75-03 re-proves results of Parts~I and~II, some of them in
a weaker form; those results are \emph{not} printed again but listed, with
the comparison, in Table~\ref{fce:kk:tab:overlap}. The rest of the report
is unchanged apart from dated notes that point here, rows added to
Tables~\ref{fce:tab:crosswalk} and~\ref{fce:tab:renamed}, the merged
research questions of Part~III and the package description of
Appendix~\ref{fce:app:package}; no existing label or number was changed.

\section{Provenance, overlap and conventions of Part~IV}\label{fce:kk:sec:intro}
\subsection{A third manuscript}\label{fce:kk:sec:source}
Source~75-03 is batch-75 manuscript~03, \emph{Fixed-Content Ballot Words.
A proof of the A215570 recurrence, a fixed-row asymptotic theorem, and
all-orders inversion}, dated October~1, 2026 and prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov (a 917-line source with 87 labels and a 22-page PDF). Archive
\texttt{OEIS\_Fixed\_Content\_Ballot\_Words.zip}, arrival commit
\texttt{4b874cea0}, placed in commit \texttt{6ea60e367}; it pins the
repository at \texttt{29aca108e} \cite{proveit75}. Its programs and data
ship with the prefix \texttt{75-03-ballot-words-}
(Section~\ref{fce:kk:sec:checks}).

Source~75-03 was written independently of sources~61 and~42: about
1.8\,\% of its 8-word shingles occur in this report (0.8\,\% of the report's
occur in it), all of them preamble, bibliography and OEIS strings. Its pin
(October~1, 19:03) predates this report's placement (\texttt{6e193dd4f},
19:16) by thirteen minutes and already contained the archives of
sources~61 and~42 as unopened zip files. Its repository check (``its
overview, combinatorics overview, and a targeted search for A215561'') could
therefore not see this report, exactly as source~61's search could not see
source~42 (Section~\ref{fce:sec:proveit}). It states that the check ``was not
an audit of every research artifact''. Its own status statement is kept:
its results ``have explicit mathematical proofs; the large recurrence
identity additionally has an exact symbolic checker. They have not been
independently peer-reviewed or formalized in Lean''; its source check of
October~1, 2026 found the statements proved here still recorded as
conjectural, but ``it is not an exhaustive priority search''; and classical
kernel, diagonal and saddle-point methods are credited rather than claimed.

Like sources~61 and~42, source~75-03 is AI-assisted and unrefereed, and no
Lean or Rocq development checks any statement of this Part. Source~75-03
outlines a staged formalization (Section~\ref{fce:sec:future}, last
subsection); none exists.

\subsection{What the third manuscript re-proves}\label{fce:kk:sec:overlap}
Table~\ref{fce:kk:tab:overlap} lists every result of source~75-03 that

third.
