Two increments over Parts~I and~II are small enough to state here.

\begin{remark}[A rate in the one-large-summand theorem (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:rem:rate}
In the notation of Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:convolution}, with $q=r$ steps of
the list \eqref{fce:lg:eq:p}, source~75-03 proves for every $F$ analytic on
a disk $|z|<R$ with $R>\Bmass$
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:rate}
 \frac{[\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol k_N}]F(\Lcal(\boldsymbol p\boldsymbol x))}{b_{\boldsymbol k_N}}
 =F'(\Bmass)+O(N^{-1/5}),
\end{equation}
an additive estimate (not a relative equivalence when $F'(\Bmass)=0$). The
inputs are the two bounds of Lemma~\ref{fce:lg:lem:bounds} with
$\varsigma=(r+1)/2$, the tail bound
$\sum_{|\boldsymbol k|>H}b_{\boldsymbol k}=O(H^{-1/2})$ (from
$\Prob(S_m=0)=O(m^{-1/2})$), and the uniform shift ratio
$b_{\boldsymbol k_N-\boldsymbol\ell}/b_{\boldsymbol k_N}
=1+O((L+L^2)/N)$ for $L=|\boldsymbol\ell|$ with $L^2/N\to0$. The cutoff
proof of Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:convolution} is run with the cutoff
$H=\lfloor N^{2/5}\rfloor$ and only for $m\le N^{1/20}$ summands, so that
$(mH)^2/N\le N^{-1/10}$; the errors are $O(H^2/N+H^{-1/2})=O(N^{-1/5})$, and
the terms $m>N^{1/20}$ are exponentially small by
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:convbound} and Cauchy's estimate. With $F=\exp$ this gives
the leading term of Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:general} with relative error
$O(N^{-1/5})$ for every finite labelled step list, and for the rows the
leading term of Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} with relative error
$O(n^{-1/5})$. Source~75-03 calls the rate ``deliberately nonsharp''; for the
rows' step set it is superseded by Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} and
Corollary~\ref{fce:cor:rays}.
\end{remark}

The limiting probability generating function \eqref{fce:lg:eq:Kpgf} is
$v$ times a negative binomial law of order two, so the number $J_N$ of
primitive factors (arches) has limiting variance
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:variance}
 \Var J_N\longrightarrow 2\kappa(\kappa-1),
\end{equation}
in the notation of Theorem~\ref{fce:lg:thm:K} (for the rows also
$\Var C_n\to2\kappa_r(\kappa_r-1)$, since $C_n=J_n-1$ in
Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:contacts});
convergence of the variance follows from the convergence of all fixed
moments proved there. Source~75-03 states this variance with the mean
$2\kappa-1$, which for $r=5$ is $14-5\sqrt5$, the last column of
Table~\ref{fce:tab:constants}.

\subsection{Conventions of Part~IV}\label{fce:kk:sec:conventions}
Part~IV uses the merged notation of Section~\ref{fce:sec:notation}.
Table~\ref{fce:tab:renamed} lists every symbol of source~75-03 that is
renamed; no normalization changed except the sign of $Q_r$. In particular
source~75-03's asymptotic coefficients $d_j$ are the $\alpha_{5,j}$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:full}, its constant $\mathcal E_r$ is $\kappa_r$, and its
excursion series $Y$ of the sextic is the $X$ of \eqref{fce:eq:sextic}.
Three readings are tempting and false:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\Lambda_n$ (source~75-03's $C_n$, the Legendre coefficient of
Theorem~\ref{fce:kk:thm:coefficient}) is not the number $C_n$ of interior
returns of Section~\ref{fce:sec:contacts}, not the functional
$\mathcal C_N$ of \eqref{fce:eq:coefficientfunctional}, and not the
lattice $\Lambda$ of Section~\ref{fce:lg:sec:allorders};
\item $\mathcal P_n(t)$ is the Legendre polynomial with
$\mathcal P_n(1)=1$, not the step polynomial $P_r(u)$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:steps}: $\mathcal P_5(t)\ne P_5(t)$;
\item $A^\circ_5(n)$ (source~75-03's $d_n$) counts the zero-free
excursions of \eqref{fce:kk:eq:zeroinsert}; it is not an asymptotic
coefficient.
\end{itemize}
The marking variables $a,b,c,d,e$ of steps $-2,\ldots,2$ are those of
\eqref{fce:eq:sextic}; the letter $a$ is never the sequence.

\section{A positive coefficient formula for A215570}\label{fce:kk:sec:formula}
\subsection{The sextic in compact form}\label{fce:kk:sec:sextic}
Put
\[
 \mathsf A=ae,\qquad \mathsf B=bd,\qquad \mathsf C=ad^2+b^2e,\qquad
 \bar c=1-c .
\]
Then \eqref{fce:eq:sextic} reads
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:sextic}
 \mathsf A^3X^6-\mathsf A^2\bar cX^5+\mathsf A(\mathsf B-\mathsf A)X^4
 +(2\mathsf A\bar c+\mathsf C)X^3+(\mathsf B-\mathsf A)X^2-\bar cX+1=0,
\end{equation}
term by term the same polynomial; source~75-03 states it in this form.

\begin{remark}[Third derivation of the sextic (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:rem:resolvent}
Let $e_1,\ldots,e_4$ be the elementary symmetric functions of the roots of
the kernel quartic $eu^4+du^3+(c-1)u^2+bu+a$. The six pair products
$u_iu_j$ ($i<j$) are the roots of the resolvent
\begin{align*}
 R(v)={}&v^6-e_2v^5+(e_1e_3-e_4)v^4
 -(e_3^2+e_1^2e_4-2e_2e_4)v^3\\
 &+e_4(e_1e_3-e_4)v^2-e_2e_4^2v+e_4^3,
\end{align*}
by expansion of $\prod_{i<j}(v-u_iu_j)$. The two small roots have product
$-aX$ (Lemma~\ref{fce:lem:kernel}). Substituting
$(e_1,e_2,e_3,e_4)=(-d/e,(c-1)/e,-b/e,a/e)$ and $v=-aX$ and multiplying by
$e^3/a^3$ gives \eqref{fce:kk:eq:sextic}, as a polynomial identity; its
$X$-derivative at the origin is $-1$, which selects the formal branch. This
is a third route beside the quadratic factorization of source~61 and the
explicit coefficient solving of source~42
(Remark~\ref{fce:rem:sextic42}); the program
\path{code/75-03-ballot-words-verify.py} checks the resolvent and its
specialization symbolically.
\end{remark}

With $U=\mathsf AX^2$, \eqref{fce:kk:eq:sextic} becomes
$(1-U)^2(1+U-\bar cX)+(1+U)\mathsf BX^2+\mathsf CX^3=0$. Since the
denominators below have constant term one, it is equivalent, in formal
power series, to
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:implicit}
 X=1+cX+\mathsf AX^2+\mathsf BX^2\frac{1+\mathsf AX^2}{(1-\mathsf AX^2)^2}
 +\mathsf CX^3\frac1{(1-\mathsf AX^2)^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Zero-step deletion and Lagrange inversion}
Let $A^\circ_5(n)$ count nonnegative excursions with $n$ copies of each of
the steps $-2,-1,1,2$ and no zero step. Removing the $n$ zero steps of an
excursion counted by $A_5(n)$ and reinserting them gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:zeroinsert}
 A_5(n)=\binom{5n}{n}A^\circ_5(n),
\end{equation}
the same deletion that gives $A_3(n)$ in Section~\ref{fce:sec:constants}.
Set $c=0$ and give $\mathsf A,\mathsf B,\mathsf C$ the weights $2,2,3$. If
$\mathfrak n=2i+2j+3k$, Lagrange inversion applied to
$\mathcal Y=zX(z^2\mathsf A,z^2\mathsf B,z^3\mathsf C)$, which by
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:implicit} satisfies
$\mathcal Y=z\bigl(1+\mathsf A\mathcal Y^2+\mathsf B\mathcal Y^2(1+\mathsf A\mathcal Y^2)(1-\mathsf A\mathcal Y^2)^{-2}+\mathsf C\mathcal Y^3(1-\mathsf A\mathcal Y^2)^{-2}\bigr)$
for an auxiliary variable $z$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:lagrange}
 [\mathsf A^i\mathsf B^j\mathsf C^k]X=
 \frac{\mathfrak n!}{j!\,k!\,(\mathfrak n+1-j-k)!}
 [u^i](1+u)^{\mathfrak n+1-k}(1-u)^{-2(j+k)}:
\end{equation}
select $j$ copies of the $\mathsf B$ term and $k$ of the $\mathsf C$ term in
the $(\mathfrak n+1)$st power of the Lagrange formula; the remaining powers
of $1+\mathsf A\mathcal Y^2$ have total exponent $\mathfrak n+1-k$, and
extracting $\mathsf A^i$ gives \eqref{fce:kk:eq:lagrange}. Equal powers of
$a,b,d,e$ force the two monomials of $\mathsf C$ to be used equally often,
say $\ell$ times each; then $i=n-\ell$, $j=n-2\ell$, $k=2\ell$, $\mathfrak
n=4n$, and $\mathsf C^{2\ell}$ contributes $\binom{2\ell}\ell$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:dsum}
 A^\circ_5(n)=\frac{(4n)!}{(3n+1)!}
 \sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
 \frac{[u^{n-\ell}](1+u)^{4n+1-2\ell}(1-u)^{-2n}}{(n-2\ell)!\,(\ell!)^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{A Legendre coefficient}
Let $\mathcal P_n(t)$ be the Legendre polynomial, $\mathcal P_n(1)=1$.
Comparing coefficients of $v^n$ in $((1-v)^2-4zv^2)^{-1/2}$ gives
\[
 \sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\frac{n!\,z^\ell}{(n-2\ell)!\,(\ell!)^2}
 =(1-4z)^{n/2}\mathcal P_n\bigl((1-4z)^{-1/2}\bigr).
\]
Substituting $z=u/(1+u)^2$ in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:dsum}, for which
$(1-4z)^{1/2}=(1-u)/(1+u)$, proves the following formula.

\begin{theorem}[Positive coefficient formula (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:thm:coefficient}
For $n\ge0$ put
\begin{align}
 \Lambda_n&=[u^n](1+u)^{3n+1}(1-u)^{-n}
             \mathcal P_n\!\left(\frac{1+u}{1-u}\right)\label{fce:kk:eq:Llegendre}\\
 &=[u^n](1+u)^{3n+1}(1-u)^{-2n}\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk^2u^k.\label{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive}
\end{align}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:AfromL}
 A_5(n)=\frac{(5n)!}{(n!)^2(3n+1)!}\,\Lambda_n .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Llegendre} and \eqref{fce:kk:eq:AfromL} follow from
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:dsum} and \eqref{fce:kk:eq:zeroinsert}. The two forms of
$\Lambda_n$ agree because
$(1-u)^n\mathcal P_n\bigl((1+u)/(1-u)\bigr)=\sum_k\binom nk^2u^k$, by the
binomial expansion of the Legendre polynomial.
\end{proof}
The first values are $\Lambda_0,\ldots,\Lambda_5=1,7,104,1935,39854,869736$,
giving $A_5(0),A_5(1),A_5(2)=1,35,18720$. Formula
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive} is a finite positive coefficient computation,
independent of the recurrence proved next. It agrees with the 17
independently enumerated terms of source~61, the 16 of source~42 and the
13 data terms of OEIS A215570; and at $n=20$ and $n=50$, where
Section~\ref{fce:sec:checks} used OEIS b-file values, its values reproduce
the ratios recorded in \path{data/61-balanced-diagnostics.json} to all 25
recorded digits (checked at the intake, 2026-10-02).

\section{Proof of Kauers--Koutschan Conjecture~15}\label{fce:kk:sec:recurrence}
Normalize
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:normalization}
 \widetilde A_5(n)=\frac{(n!)^3((n+1)!)^2}{(5n)!}A_5(n)=\mathfrak h_n\Lambda_n,
 \qquad \mathfrak h_n=\frac{n!\,((n+1)!)^2}{(3n+1)!},
\end{equation}
and put
\begin{align*}
 \mathfrak R(n)&=65n^3+593n^2+1772n+1740,\\
 \mathfrak S(n)&=65n^3+398n^2+781n+496,\\
 \mathfrak U(n)&=2015n^5+24428n^4+114387n^3+258294n^2+281088n+118368,\\
 \mathfrak V(n)&=910n^5+11032n^4+52047n^3+119686n^2+134365n+58980.
\end{align*}

\begin{theorem}[Kauers--Koutschan Conjecture~15 (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:thm:recurrence}
For every integer $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:prec}
 \sum_{j=0}^3\mathfrak p_j(n)\widetilde A_5(n+j)=0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak p_0(n)&=-2(n+1)(n+2)\mathfrak R(n),&
 \mathfrak p_1(n)&=\mathfrak U(n),\notag\\
 \mathfrak p_2(n)&=-4\mathfrak V(n),&
 \mathfrak p_3(n)&=3(3n+8)(3n+10)\mathfrak S(n).\label{fce:kk:eq:ps}
\end{align}
Equivalently, with $K=\{1,\ldots,14\}\setminus\{5,10\}$, the sequence
satisfies the recurrence displayed in OEIS A215570 \cite{oeis5}:
\begin{align}
 &3(n+1)(n+2)^3(n+3)^4(n+4)^2(3n+8)(3n+10)\,\mathfrak S(n)\,A_5(n+3)\notag\\
 &\;-20(n+1)(n+2)^3(n+3)^2\textstyle\prod_{k=11}^{14}(5n+k)\,\mathfrak V(n)\,A_5(n+2)\notag\\
 &\;+25(n+1)(n+2)\textstyle\prod_{k\in K,\,k\ge6}(5n+k)\,\mathfrak U(n)\,A_5(n+1)\notag\\
 &\;-250\textstyle\prod_{k\in K}(5n+k)\,\mathfrak R(n)\,A_5(n)=0.\label{fce:kk:eq:oeisform}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}
The form \eqref{fce:kk:eq:oeisform}, of order three and degree~15, is the
one Kauers and Koutschan guessed \cite{kk}; source~75-03 proves the
normalized form \eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec} and calls its coefficients ``the
coefficients in Conjecture~15'' without printing the unnormalized operator.
The two are equivalent: with
$w(n)=(n!)^3((n+1)!)^2/(5n)!$ and
\[
 \mathfrak g(n)=\frac{125\prod_{k\in K}(5n+k)}{(n+1)(n+2)},
\]
the coefficient of $A_5(n+j)$ in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:oeisform} is
$\mathfrak g(n)\,\mathfrak p_j(n)\,w(n+j)/w(n)$ for $j=0,1,2,3$, so
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:oeisform} is $\mathfrak g(n)/w(n)$ times
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec}, and $\mathfrak g(n)\ne0$. This was checked
symbolically at the intake (2026-10-02); the OEIS operator also annihilates
the terms given by \eqref{fce:kk:eq:AfromL} for $0\le n\le59$.

\subsection{A rational residue representation}
The substitution $t=(1+u)/(1-u)$ in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:Llegendre} gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:residue}
 \Lambda_n=\Res_{t=1}\mathcal W_n(t)\mathcal P_n(t),\qquad
 \mathcal W_n(t)=\frac{2^{2n+2}t^{3n+1}}{(t-1)^{n+1}(t+1)^{n+2}},
\end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:ratioL}
 \frac{\mathcal W_{n+j}}{\mathcal W_n}=\left(\frac{4t^3}{t^2-1}\right)^j,
 \qquad \mathsf L_n(t):=\frac{\mathcal W_n'}{\mathcal W_n}=
 \frac{3n+1}{t}-\frac{n+1}{t-1}-\frac{n+2}{t+1}.
\end{equation}
The factor $(t+1)$ in the denominator of $\mathcal W_n$ is essential. The
coefficients
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak q_0(n)&=-18(n+1)(3n+2)(3n+4)(3n+5)(3n+7)\mathfrak R(n),\notag\\
 \mathfrak q_1(n)&=3(n+2)(3n+5)(3n+7)\mathfrak U(n),\notag\\
 \mathfrak q_2(n)&=-4(n+2)(n+3)^2\mathfrak V(n),\notag\\
 \mathfrak q_3(n)&=(n+2)(n+3)^2(n+4)^2\mathfrak S(n)\label{fce:kk:eq:qs}
\end{align}
satisfy, by cancellation of factorials,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:pqrelation}
 \mathfrak q_j(n)=\mathfrak c(n)\,\mathfrak p_j(n)\frac{\mathfrak h_{n+j}}{\mathfrak h_n},\qquad
 \mathfrak c(n)=\frac{9(3n+2)(3n+4)(3n+5)(3n+7)}{n+2},
\end{equation}
with all denominators nonzero for $n\ge0$.

\subsection{The certificate identity}
Define vectors of rational functions
\[
 \boldsymbol\zeta_0=\binom10,\quad \boldsymbol\zeta_1=\binom01,\quad
 \boldsymbol\zeta_{j+1}=\frac{(2(n+j)+1)t\,\boldsymbol\zeta_j-(n+j)\boldsymbol\zeta_{j-1}}{n+j+1}
 \quad(j=1,2).
\]
By the Legendre recurrence,
$\mathcal P_{n+j}=\zeta_{j,0}\mathcal P_n+\zeta_{j,1}\mathcal P_{n+1}$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:Rvector}
 \binom{\mathscr R_0}{\mathscr R_1}
 =\sum_{j=0}^3\mathfrak q_j(n)\left(\frac{4t^3}{t^2-1}\right)^j\boldsymbol\zeta_j .
\end{equation}
The rational functions $\mathscr U_n(t),\mathscr V_n(t)$ printed in
Appendix~\ref{fce:kk:app:certificate} satisfy the two identities in
$\Q(n,t)$
\begin{align}
 \mathscr R_0&=\mathscr U_n'+\mathsf L_n\mathscr U_n
       -\frac{(n+1)t}{t^2-1}\mathscr U_n-\frac{n+1}{t^2-1}\mathscr V_n,
       \label{fce:kk:eq:cert1}\\
 \mathscr R_1&=\mathscr V_n'+\mathsf L_n\mathscr V_n
       +\frac{n+1}{t^2-1}\mathscr U_n+\frac{(n+1)t}{t^2-1}\mathscr V_n .
       \label{fce:kk:eq:cert2}
\end{align}
This is an explicit finite certificate: it is verified by substituting the
printed polynomials and clearing denominators.
\path{code/75-03-ballot-words-verify.py} does exactly this with exact
rational coefficients (\texttt{sympy.cancel} of both residuals over
$\Q(n,t)$), with no numerical sampling.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{fce:kk:thm:recurrence}]
The Legendre derivative identities
\[
 \mathcal P_n'=\frac{n+1}{t^2-1}(\mathcal P_{n+1}-t\mathcal P_n),\qquad
 \mathcal P_{n+1}'=\frac{n+1}{t^2-1}(t\mathcal P_{n+1}-\mathcal P_n)
\]
show that \eqref{fce:kk:eq:cert1}--\eqref{fce:kk:eq:cert2} are precisely
the coefficients of $\mathcal P_n$ and $\mathcal P_{n+1}$ in the scalar
identity
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:telescoper}
 \sum_{j=0}^3\mathfrak q_j(n)\mathcal W_{n+j}(t)\mathcal P_{n+j}(t)
 =\frac{d}{dt}\Bigl[\mathcal W_n(t)
       \bigl(\mathscr U_n(t)\mathcal P_n(t)+\mathscr V_n(t)\mathcal P_{n+1}(t)\bigr)\Bigr].
\end{equation}
For each $n\ge0$ every function here is rational in $t$; the only
parameter denominator of the certificate is $n+2$. The residue at a finite
point of the derivative of a rational function is zero. Taking the residue
at $t=1$ and using \eqref{fce:kk:eq:residue} gives
$\sum_{j=0}^3\mathfrak q_j(n)\Lambda_{n+j}=0$. Substituting
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:pqrelation} and \eqref{fce:kk:eq:normalization} and
cancelling $\mathfrak c(n)/\mathfrak h_n$ yields \eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec} for
every $n\ge0$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact generation, and what is not asserted}
The leading coefficients $\mathfrak p_3(n)$ and $\mathfrak q_3(n)$ are
positive for $n\ge0$, so the recurrence and $\Lambda_0,\Lambda_1,\Lambda_2$
determine the sequence. Working with the integers $\Lambda_n$ avoids the
rational normalization; \eqref{fce:kk:eq:AfromL} recovers $A_5(n)$. Each new
term costs a bounded number of arithmetic operations; this is not a
constant bit-cost claim, since the integers have linearly growing bit
length.

The order is three, as conjectured. No claim of minimality over every
coefficient field is made. The normalized recurrence has the exact
solution $3n+2$:
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:linearsolution}
 \sum_{j=0}^3\mathfrak p_j(n)\bigl(3(n+j)+2\bigr)=0,
\end{equation}
which is why a leading asymptotic alone does not determine every formal
coefficient: a second asymptotic datum is needed
(Section~\ref{fce:kk:sec:coefficients}).

This settles Kauers and Koutschan's Conjecture~15 \cite{kk}, listed as
open in Section~\ref{fce:sec:future}. Their request for a provably correct
recurrence for A215562 ($r=4$) remains open. The route is not the creative
telescoping of the sextic proposed there (Remark~\ref{fce:rem:sextic42}):
zero-step deletion and Lagrange inversion reduce the diagonal to the
one-variable coefficient \eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive}, and the telescoping
is done on its Legendre residue representation.

\section{The exact coefficients \texorpdfstring{$\alpha_{5,2},\ldots,\alpha_{5,8}$}{alpha(5,2),...,alpha(5,8)}}\label{fce:kk:sec:coefficients}
\subsection{A second route to the expansion, a third to \texorpdfstring{$\alpha_{5,1}$}{alpha(5,1)}}\label{fce:kk:sec:saddle}
The existence of a complete expansion \eqref{fce:eq:full} for $r=5$ with
$\alpha_{5,j}\in\Q(\sqrt5)$ is Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} with
Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:five}. Source~75-03 proves it again from
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive}, by a saddle point in two variables instead of
three marker variables. Since
$\ct_v(1+v)^n(1+u/v)^n=\sum_k\binom nk^2u^k$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:constantterm}
 \Lambda_n=\ct_{u,v}(1+u)\,\Pi(u,v)^n,\qquad
 \Pi(u,v)=\frac{(1+u)^3(1+v)(1+u/v)}{u(1-u)^2}.
\end{equation}
The logarithmic gradient of $\Pi$ vanishes at
$(u_0,v_0)=(\varphi^{-4},\varphi^{-2})$, $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$: the $v$
equation gives $v^2=u$, and with $u=t^2$, $v=t$ the $u$ equation becomes
$(t+1)(t^2-3t+1)=0$, whose root in $(0,1)$ is $t=\varphi^{-2}=(3-\sqrt5)/2$.
There
\[
 \Pi(u_0,v_0)=27,\qquad 1+u_0=3\varphi^{-2},\qquad
 H_\Pi=\begin{pmatrix}14/15&-1/5\\-1/5&2/5\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \det H_\Pi=\frac13,
\]
$H_\Pi$ being the Hessian of $\log\Pi$ in logarithmic coordinates. On the
torus $|u|=u_0$, $|v|=v_0$ one has $|\Pi|\le27$ with equality only at
$(u_0,v_0)$ (the triangle inequalities for $1+u$ and $1+v$ already force
both phases to vanish, and $|1-u|\ge1-u_0$). The standard saddle argument
(restriction to angular distance $n^{-2/5}$, a Gaussian majorant inside and
an $O(27^ne^{-cn^{1/5}})$ bound outside) gives, at every finite order,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:Lasympt}
 \Lambda_n=\frac{3\sqrt3\,\varphi^{-2}}{2\pi}\frac{27^n}{n}
 \left(1+\frac{\eta^\Lambda_1}{n}+\frac{\eta^\Lambda_2}{n^2}+\cdots\right),
 \qquad \eta^\Lambda_j\in\Q(\sqrt5),
\end{equation}
and with Stirling's series in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:AfromL} the expansion
\eqref{fce:eq:full} for $r=5$, with the constant $K_5$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:fivecorrection}.

For the first correction put, for $j\ge2$,
$\ell^+_j(q)=(q\partial_q)^j\log(1+q)$ and
$\ell^-_j(q)=(q\partial_q)^j[-\log(1-q)]$, and
\[
 \mathcal K_j(Z_1,Z_2)=\bigl(3\ell^+_j(u_0)+2\ell^-_j(u_0)\bigr)Z_1^j
 +\ell^+_j(\varphi^{-2})\bigl(Z_2^j+(Z_1-Z_2)^j\bigr),
\]
where $(Z_1,Z_2)$ is a centred Gaussian vector with covariance
$H_\Pi^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}6/5&3/5\\3/5&14/5\end{pmatrix}$. Taylor expansion
of amplitude and exponent gives, with $G_0=1+u_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:eta}
 \eta^\Lambda_1=\frac1{G_0}\Exp\left[
 -\frac{u_0Z_1^2}{2}+\frac{u_0Z_1\mathcal K_3}{6}
 +\frac{G_0\mathcal K_4}{24}-\frac{G_0\mathcal K_3^2}{72}\right]
 =-\frac{71}{90}+\frac{13\sqrt5}{50}.
\end{equation}
The factorial multiplier in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:AfromL} has relative first
correction $-23/45$, so $\alpha_{5,1}=\eta^\Lambda_1-23/45
=-13/10+13\sqrt5/50$: the value of \eqref{fce:eq:fivecorrection}, here by a
third contraction after Sections~\ref{fce:sec:correction}
and~\ref{fce:lg:sec:first}. The checker evaluates \eqref{fce:kk:eq:eta}
exactly.

\subsection{The triangular algorithm}
By \eqref{fce:eq:full} and Stirling's series,
$\widetilde A_5(n)$ has a complete expansion
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:tildeinitial}
 \widetilde A_5(n)\sim n\sum_{k\ge0}\tilde c_kn^{-k},\qquad
 \tilde c_0=\varphi^{-2},\qquad \tilde c_1=1-\frac{4\sqrt5}{25},
\end{equation}
where $\tilde c_0$ comes from $K_5$ and $\tilde c_1=\varphi^{-2}(\eta^\Lambda_1+17/9)$
from $\alpha_{5,1}$, $17/9$ being the first Stirling correction of
$\mathfrak h_n$. Put $\widehat{\mathfrak p}_j(z)=z^5\mathfrak p_j(1/z)$.
Substituting the expansion in \eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec} with $n=1/z$ gives,
for every $k\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:ckalgorithm}
 [z^{k+2}]\sum_{j=0}^3\widehat{\mathfrak p}_j(z)
       \sum_{\ell=0}^k\tilde c_\ell z^\ell(1+jz)^{1-\ell}=0 .
\end{equation}
For a trial term $n^\beta$ the first nonzero coefficient is
$[z^2]\sum_j\widehat{\mathfrak p}_j(z)(1+jz)^\beta=1625\beta(\beta-1)$, so
the two free powers are $n$ and $1$, and the coefficient of $\tilde c_k$ in
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:ckalgorithm} is $1625k(k-1)\ne0$. Given $\tilde c_0$ and
$\tilde c_1$, \eqref{fce:kk:eq:ckalgorithm} is therefore triangular; for
example $\tilde c_2=2\sqrt5/25$, $\tilde c_3=-74\sqrt5/3125$,
$\tilde c_4=814\sqrt5/78125$. Finally, as formal series in $z$,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:dalgorithm}
 \sum_{k\ge0}\alpha_{5,k}z^k=
 \frac{\sum_{k\ge0}\tilde c_kz^k}{\varphi^{-2}(1+z)^2}
 \exp\left(\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{B_{2j}(5^{1-2j}-5)}{2j(2j-1)}z^{2j-1}\right),
\end{equation}
with Bernoulli numbers $B_{2j}$: the exponential is Stirling's correction
for $(5n)!/(n!)^5$, and $(1+z)^{-2}$ that of $(n+1)^{-2}$, since
$A_5(n)=\widetilde A_5(n)(5n)!/((n!)^5(n+1)^2)$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact coefficients for A215570 (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:prop:alpha}
For every $k$, the coefficient $\alpha_{5,k}$ of \eqref{fce:eq:full} is the
$k$th coefficient computed by \eqref{fce:kk:eq:ckalgorithm} and
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:dalgorithm} from \eqref{fce:kk:eq:tildeinitial}. The values
for $k\le8$ are those of Table~\ref{fce:kk:tab:alpha}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The expansion exists (Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main}, or
Section~\ref{fce:kk:sec:saddle}); inserting it into
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec}, which holds for every $n$, gives a formal identity
whose coefficients are \eqref{fce:kk:eq:ckalgorithm}. These determine
$\tilde c_k$ for $k\ge2$ uniquely from $\tilde c_0,\tilde c_1$, which are
fixed by $K_5$ and $\alpha_{5,1}$ (Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:five}). Stirling's
series converts $\tilde c_k$ into $\alpha_{5,k}$ by
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:dalgorithm}. The table entries are the exact values
recorded by \path{code/75-03-ballot-words-verify.py} in
\path{data/75-03-ballot-words-verification_results.json}, regenerated
identically at the intake.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Scope (source~75-03)]\label{fce:kk:rem:scope}
Proposition~\ref{fce:kk:prop:alpha} concerns the dominant exponential
sector, as in the remark closing Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}; it determines
no exponentially small sector and no Stokes data. The leading characteristic
polynomial of the normalized recurrence \eqref{fce:kk:eq:prec} is
$65(\lambda-1)^2(27\lambda-2)$, here with $\lambda$ a characteristic root,
not $r\log r$. The double root $1$ carries the two powers $n$ and $1$
above; the third root $2/27$ indicates a possible formal mode smaller by
the factor $(2/27)^n$, whose contribution to the sequence is not determined
here. The coefficients grow from $k=6$ on, as expected of an asymptotic
series; nothing is claimed about its summability.
\end{remark}

\section{Inversion, rounding and repository machinery}\label{fce:kk:sec:inverse}
Source~75-03's inversion of A215570 is the instance $r=5$ of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:inverse} and of the rounding bound
\eqref{fce:lg:eq:rounding}, and through them of the canonical transseries
volume (Section~\ref{fce:sec:repository}): its Lambert anchor is
\eqref{fce:eq:lambertcore}, the large-solution case $b<0$ of
\texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} (display \texttt{p0:eq:core-lambert-minus},
with $a=\lambda=5\log5$ and $b=-\beta=-3$); its corrections are the first two
coefficients of the Lambert-centred reversion
\texttt{p0:thm:lambert-centered}, which is the explicit inverse
\texttt{t2:thm:balanced-inverse} (display \texttt{t2:eq:balanced-first}) of
the companion volume
\path{Analysis/Transseries/docs/series-and-transseries/Combinatorial_Transseries_Inverses/Combinatorial_Transseries_Inverses.tex};
and its rounded threshold is parts~(2)--(3) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase}
(Remark~\ref{fce:lg:rem:staircase}). Source~75-03 cites none of these, and
no novelty is claimed for them. What is new is one value: with
$\alpha_{5,2}$ known, \eqref{fce:eq:inversefirst} for $r=5$ is explicit to
second order,
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:inversefive}
 n=x_0+\frac{13(5-\sqrt5)}{50\lambda}\frac1{x_0}
 +\left(\frac{83\sqrt5-213}{500\lambda}+\frac{39(5-\sqrt5)}{50\lambda^2}\right)\frac1{x_0^2}
 +O(x_0^{-3}),
\end{equation}
for $Y=A_5(n)$, $x_0=x_0(Y)$, $\lambda=5\log5$; here
$b_2=\alpha_{5,2}-\tfrac12\alpha_{5,1}^2=(213-83\sqrt5)/500$, and the two
coefficients are $\mu_1=0.0893010312$ and $\mu_2=0.0264800992$.
(2026-10-05 note: source~101-86 derives the same second-order inverse from its own,
recurrence-free $\alpha_{5,2}$, Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:monotone}.) The second
term accounts for the residual column of the inverse diagnostic table in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:checks}: $\mu_2/x_0^2$ is $4.150\cdot10^{-4}$,
$1.035\cdot10^{-4}$ and $1.059\cdot10^{-5}$ at $n=8,16,50$, against the
printed $-4.164\cdot10^{-4}$, $-1.038\cdot10^{-4}$, $-1.060\cdot10^{-5}$
(the column is $-\mu_2/x_0^2+O(x_0^{-3})$). Source~75-03 states the rounded
threshold
\[
 \bigl\lceil x_*(Y)-Cx_0^{-3}\bigr\rceil\le N_5(Y)\le\bigl\lceil x_*(Y)+Cx_0^{-3}\bigr\rceil,
\]
with $x_*$ the truncation of \eqref{fce:kk:eq:inversefive} after $x_0^{-2}$;
this is \eqref{fce:lg:eq:rounding} with $M=2$, since $x_0^{-3}$ and
$(\log Y)^{-3}$ are comparable. It also carries the reversion of the anchor
one order beyond \eqref{fce:lg:eq:loginverse}: with
$D_Y=\log(Y/K_5)-3\log\lambda$, the number $u=\lambda x_0$ solves
$u-3\log u=D_Y$, and
\begin{equation}\label{fce:kk:eq:loganchor}
 \lambda x_0=D_Y+3\log D_Y+\frac{9\log D_Y}{D_Y}
 +\frac{27\log D_Y-\tfrac{27}{2}(\log D_Y)^2}{D_Y^2}
 +O\!\left(\frac{(\log D_Y)^3}{D_Y^3}\right),
\end{equation}
the nested-logarithm expansion that follows \texttt{t2:thm:balanced-inverse}
in the companion volume. Source~75-03 adds that for other rows the general
theorem ``currently supplies only the coarser $O(x^{-1/5})$ uncertainty'';
in this report Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} gives every row a complete
expansion, so Section~\ref{fce:sec:inverse} applies to every row at every
order.

\section{Source 75-03's computations}\label{fce:kk:sec:checks}
The shipped \path{code/75-03-ballot-words-verify.py}, with its integer
certificate arrays in \path{code/75-03-ballot-words-certificate_data.py},
performs these exact checks:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item the generic quartic pair-product resolvent of
Remark~\ref{fce:kk:rem:resolvent} and its specialization to the sextic, as
polynomial identities;
\item both certificate identities \eqref{fce:kk:eq:cert1}--\eqref{fce:kk:eq:cert2}
identically in $\Q(n,t)$, the four relations \eqref{fce:kk:eq:pqrelation},
and the solution \eqref{fce:kk:eq:linearsolution};
\item the Hessian $H_\Pi$ and the Gaussian expectation \eqref{fce:kk:eq:eta}
in $\Q(\sqrt5)$;
\item agreement of the recurrence-generated $\Lambda_n$ with
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive} for $0\le n\le103$;
\item an independent content-state dynamic program, the recursion of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:checks} (no row recurrence is used), for $r=2,3,4$
($n\le10$), $r=5$ ($n\le8$), $r=6$ ($n\le5$) and $r=7$ ($n\le4$), and the
thirteen OEIS data terms of A215570.
\end{enumerate}
It also generates $\Lambda_n$ for $n\le1000$ by the recurrence, writes
$\Lambda_n$ and $A_5(n)$ for $n\le103$ to
\path{data/75-03-ballot-words-a215570_terms.csv}, and records
$\alpha_{5,0},\ldots,\alpha_{5,8}$, $\tilde c_0,\ldots,\tilde c_8$, the
constants of Table~\ref{fce:tab:constants} for $r\le8$ (to 35 digits),
the dynamic-programming rows and Table~\ref{fce:kk:tab:errors} in
\path{data/75-03-ballot-words-verification_results.json}; its standard output
is \path{data/75-03-ballot-words-verification_log.txt}. The recorded run used
Python~3.13.5, SymPy~1.14.0 and mpmath~1.3.0 and took 3.6~s. The 53
dynamic-programming terms equal source~61's enumerations
(\path{data/61-balanced-exact_rows.json}). The intake reran the program on a
copy (Python~3.14.4): all six checks passed, the CSV was regenerated
byte for byte, and the JSON differs only in the recorded Python version and
running time (and in its line endings: on Windows the program writes it
with CRLF, while the shipped file has LF).

Source~75-03 marks the boundaries of these checks. The certificate check is
finite symbolic algebra: it does not replace the derivation from words to
\eqref{fce:kk:eq:Lpositive}, which is the proof above, and ``finite
agreement alone would not prove the conjecture''. Numerical errors are
high-precision illustrations, not interval enclosures. ``No Lean files are
supplied as if they had been compiled.''

\part{Equal-content ballot words: the first correction for A215562}\label{fce:ba:part}

Part~V was added on October~2, 2026, in batch~77 of ProveIt's
incoming-reports intake, from a fourth manuscript on the same problem
(source~77-44), after Part~IV. Source~77-44 proves the leading constants of
A215562 and A215570, every fixed order of their expansions, the fixed-row
theorem for every alphabet and D-finiteness: a \emph{fourth} independent
proof of Theorems~\ref{fce:thm:main} and~\ref{fce:thm:class} (its
D-finiteness part) and of the constructions of Sections~4--7, with
identical constants and the same sextic. Those results are not printed
again; Table~\ref{fce:ba:tab:overlap} lists them. What it adds is one new
value, the first correction $\alpha_{4,1}$ of A215562
(Theorem~\ref{fce:ba:thm:alpha41}), which meets the target stated in
Part~III (Section~\ref{fce:sec:future}); an operator-norm proof of the
global-arc bound (Section~\ref{fce:ba:sec:global}), a second route to
Section~6.4; the explicit Hessian for the fourth row; a nested-logarithm
inverse for every row (Remark~\ref{fce:ba:rem:inverse}); and two literature
pointers (Section~\ref{fce:ba:sec:literature}). The rest of the report is
unchanged apart from dated notes that point here, rows added to
Tables~\ref{fce:tab:crosswalk} and~\ref{fce:tab:renamed}, and the package
description of Appendix~\ref{fce:app:package}; no existing label or number
was changed.

\section{Provenance, overlap and conventions of Part~V}\label{fce:ba:sec:intro}
\subsection{A fourth manuscript}\label{fce:ba:sec:source}
Source~77-44 is batch-77 manuscript~44, \emph{Equal-content ballot words:
leading constants and fixed-order expansions for OEIS A215562 and A215570},
dated October~1, 2026, author line ``Research note with independently
reviewed proofs'' (a 594-line source with 18 labels and an 8-page PDF).
Archive \path{oeis-ballot-asymptotics-20261001.zip}, arrival commit
\texttt{096ee7b87}, placed in commit \texttt{aa7345800}. It names no ProveIt
commit and records no repository search. Its programs and receipts ship
with the prefix \texttt{77-44-bal-} (Section~\ref{fce:ba:sec:checks}).

Source~77-44 was written without knowledge of this report: its receipts
record the UTC date October~1, 2026, and its archive members are stamped
10:40--10:46 (no time zone), in any time zone before this report's
placement (\texttt{6e193dd4f}, October~2, 02:16 UTC). Its Theorem~1, Corollary~2, Lemmas~3
and~4, Proposition~5 and Theorem~6 re-prove Theorems~\ref{fce:thm:main}
and~\ref{fce:thm:class} and Sections~4--7 of this report and are listed in
Table~\ref{fce:ba:tab:overlap}, not reprinted. It presents the row-wise
asymptotic recorded conjecturally in A215561 as a consequence of its own
argument; in this report its proof is the fourth (after sources~61, 42
and~75-03). Its status statement is kept: ``The proofs have been
independently reviewed and the supporting computations reproduced. These
are ordinary mathematical proofs, not machine-checked theorems or claims of
publication priority. A complete exponentially improved transseries is not
claimed.'' The review it refers to is its own pre-delivery review (an
approval receipt), not refereeing; the delivery says nothing about AI
assistance. Its constants ``exist for every fixed $J$; this draft does not
give numerical thresholds or optimized numerical remainder constants''. No
Lean or Rocq development checks any statement of this Part.

Source~77-44 calls the Kauers--Koutschan recurrence for A215570 ``guessed''
and unproved, and lists ``proving the particular guessed recurrence'' among
its future questions. (2026-10-02 note: the recurrence is proved in
Part~\ref{fce:kk:part}, Theorem~\ref{fce:kk:thm:recurrence}; the A215562
recurrence remains open, Section~\ref{fce:sec:future}.)

\subsection{Conventions of Part~V}\label{fce:ba:sec:conventions}
Part~V uses the merged notation of Section~\ref{fce:sec:notation};
Table~\ref{fce:tab:renamed} lists every renamed symbol of source~77-44. Its
alphabet size $q$ is $r$ here, and its normalized coefficients $c_{q,j}$ are
the $\alpha_{r,j}$ of \eqref{fce:eq:full}. One normalization differs: its
step polynomial $P_q(u)=\sum_iu^{s_i}$ is $r$ times the $P_r$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:steps}, so its dominant singularity is at $t=1/q$, not $t=1$.
Three readings are tempting and false:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\varrho=\varphi-\sqrt\varphi=\kappa_4/4$ (source~77-44's $r$) is not
the alphabet size $r$; it is the product $q$ of the two interior roots in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:constants}, the root in $(0,1)$ of
$\varrho^4-2\varrho^3-2\varrho^2-2\varrho+1$.
\item $c_{q,j}$ is not the OEIS-style constant $c(r)$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:constant}, and source~77-44's $c=-\min S_q$ is the depth $h$.
\item Source~77-44's $\mathcal B$ is the bridge generating function
$B(t;\boldsymbol x)$, not the length correction $\mathcal B$ of
\eqref{fce:eq:lengthcorrection}, which it calls $D_{\rm uni}$.
\end{itemize}

\section{The first correction for A215562}\label{fce:ba:sec:alpha41}
\begin{theorem}[First correction of the fourth row (source~77-44)]\label{fce:ba:thm:alpha41}
Let $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ and $\varrho=\varphi-\sqrt\varphi$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:four}
 A_4(n)=\frac{\varrho\,256^n}{\sqrt2\,\pi^{3/2}n^{5/2}}
 \left(1+\frac{\alpha_{4,1}}n+O(n^{-2})\right),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:alpha41}
 \alpha_{4,1}=-\frac{37\varrho^3}{200}+\frac{11\varrho^2}{40}
              +\frac{13\varrho}{40}-\frac{259}{400}
            =-0.509784684867675\ldots .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The leading factor is $K_4\,4^{4n}n^{-5/2}$ of \eqref{fce:eq:full}, with
$\kappa_4=4\varrho$; it is the formula credited to Kotesovec in the OEIS
\cite{oeis4}. Every $\alpha_{4,j}$ lies in $\Q(\varrho)$, a quartic field,
by the argument that gives $\alpha_{5,j}\in\Q(\sqrt5)$ in
Section~\ref{fce:sec:correction}; source~77-44 states that all its
normalized coefficients are effectively computable real algebraic numbers.
\begin{proof}[Proof (source~77-44)]
Apply \eqref{fce:eq:alphafirst} with $r=4$: $\alpha_{4,1}=(\mathcal B+\mathcal
S)/4+(1/4-4)/12$. In source~77-44's form, the length part is
\[
 \mathcal B=-\frac32d_2+\frac12\alpha^2,\qquad
 \alpha^2=\frac{2q}{P_q''(1)},\quad
 \beta=-\frac{P_q'''(1)\alpha^2}{6P_q''(1)},\quad
 d_2=1+\beta+q\sum_{\substack{P_q(r_j)=q\\|r_j|<1}}\frac1{r_jP_q'(r_j)},
\]
where $u_1=1-\alpha\eta^{1/2}+\beta\eta+\cdots$ is the coalescing small
root at unit marks, $\eta=1-t/\rho$, and $d_2$ is the coefficient of $\eta$
in $\E/E_*$; for $q=4$, $P_4(u)=u^{-3}+u^{-1}+u+u^3$, $P_4''(1)=20$ and
$\alpha^2=2/5=2/V$. The saddle part $\mathcal S$ is
\eqref{fce:eq:saddlecorrection}, written by source~77-44 as
\[
 \mathcal S=-\frac12\sum_{ij}G_{ij}\bigl(g_{ij}+g_ig_j+2(\partial_i\log b)g_j\bigr)
 +\frac12\sum_{ijkl}g_i\kappa_{jkl}G_{ij}G_{kl},
\]
with $G=H^{-1}$, $g=\log E_*(e^{\boldsymbol z})$ and $b=V(e^{\boldsymbol z})^{-1/2}$
in the logarithmic marks $\boldsymbol z$ of the first two letters. For $r=4$
the marked steps are $-3,-1$, the unmarked ones $1,3$, and
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:H4}
 H_4=\begin{pmatrix}3/40&-1/10\\-1/10&7/40\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \det H_4=\frac1{320}.
\end{equation}
Direct implicit differentiation of the kernel roots, in $\Q(\varrho)$ reduced
modulo $\varrho^4-2\varrho^3-2\varrho^2-2\varrho+1$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:BS}
 \mathcal B=-\frac{6\varrho^3}{25}+\frac{3\varrho^2}{10}+\frac{3\varrho}5-\frac{47}{50},
 \qquad
 \mathcal S=-\frac{\varrho^3}2+\frac{4\varrho^2}5+\frac{7\varrho}{10}-\frac25,
\end{equation}
and \eqref{fce:eq:alphafirst} yields \eqref{fce:ba:eq:alpha41}.
\end{proof}
Source~77-44's exact script \path{code/77-44-bal-ballot_four_correction.py}
implements this computation; its numerical-derivative check
(\path{code/77-44-bal-verify_ballot_numerical_derivatives.py}) differentiates
numerical implicit root solves directly, without the symbolic derivative
formulas, and reproduces $\alpha_{4,1}$ and $\alpha_{5,1}$ to at least 54
decimal digits.

\begin{remark}[Checks made at the merge]\label{fce:ba:rem:checks}
The intake checked that \eqref{fce:ba:eq:BS} with \eqref{fce:eq:alphafirst}
gives exactly \eqref{fce:ba:eq:alpha41} ($(\mathcal B+\mathcal
S)/4=-\frac{37}{200}\varrho^3+\frac{11}{40}\varrho^2+\frac{13}{40}\varrho-\frac{67}{200}$
and $(1/4-4)/12=-\frac5{16}$), and that $\mathcal B\approx-0.706416072$ and
$\mathcal S\approx-0.082722667$. Richardson extrapolation of
$n\bigl(A_4(n)/(K_4\,256^nn^{-5/2})-1\bigr)$ on the exact terms $n\le30$, which equal
the enumerations of Section~\ref{fce:sec:checks} on their common range,
gives $-0.5097844658$, $-0.5097846437$ and $-0.5097846764$ at orders $4$, $6$
and $8$: agreement with \eqref{fce:ba:eq:alpha41} to eight digits. The
same procedure reproduces $\alpha_{5,1}$ to thirteen digits. These are
diagnostics, not part of the proof. $\det H_4=1/320$ and the five-letter
matrix $H_5$ of source~77-44, with $\det H_5=1/6250$, are those of
\eqref{fce:eq:detH} and Section~\ref{fce:sec:correction}.
\end{remark}

With $\alpha_{4,1}$ known, \eqref{fce:eq:inversefirst} is explicit to first
order for the fourth row (this display was added at the merge):
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:inversefour}
 n=x_0+\frac{|\alpha_{4,1}|}{4\log4}\frac1{x_0}+O(x_0^{-2}),\qquad
 \frac{|\alpha_{4,1}|}{4\log4}=0.0919329795\ldots,\qquad Y=A_4(n),
\end{equation}
with $x_0=x_0(Y)$ the Lambert core \eqref{fce:eq:lambertcore} for
$(K,\lambda,\beta)=(K_4,4\log4,5/2)$.

\section{A second route to the global-arc bound}\label{fce:ba:sec:global}
Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle} (``Why other phases do not contribute'') shows
that the marker torus contributes only near $\boldsymbol z=\one$ by
continuing the small-root product to a slightly larger disk away from that
point. Source~77-44 proves the needed bound directly on coefficients.

\begin{lemma}[Global mark arcs (source~77-44)]\label{fce:ba:lem:global}
Let $E_N(\boldsymbol w)$ and $B_N(\boldsymbol w)$ be the mark-weighted counts
of nonnegative excursions and zero-height bridges of length $N$, with the
first $r-2$ step types marked by $\boldsymbol w$ and the unnormalized step
polynomial $P(u;\boldsymbol w)$. For $\boldsymbol w$ on the unit torus,
\[
 |E_N(\boldsymbol w)|\le\Bigl(\max_{|u|=1}|P(u;\boldsymbol w)|\Bigr)^N,
 \qquad
 |B_N(\boldsymbol w)|\le\Bigl(\max_{|u|=1}|P(u;\boldsymbol w)|\Bigr)^N .
\]
On any closed subset of the torus excluding $\boldsymbol1$ the maximum is at
most $r-\epsilon$ for some $\epsilon>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (source~77-44)]
The bridge inequality follows from its Fourier constant-term integral. For
excursions, let $L_{\boldsymbol w}$ be the bilateral Laurent operator on
$\ell^2(\Z)$ with the marked steps as its coefficients. Fourier
transformation gives $\|L_{\boldsymbol w}\|=\max_{|u|=1}|P(u;\boldsymbol w)|$.
If $\Pi$ projects onto heights $0,1,\ldots$, then $T=\Pi L_{\boldsymbol w}\Pi$
is the killed transfer operator and $E_N=\langle e_0,T^Ne_0\rangle$. Thus
$|E_N|\le\|T\|^N\le\|L_{\boldsymbol w}\|^N$.

Equality in the triangle bound $|P(u;\boldsymbol w)|\le r$ requires all its
unit-modulus summands to have the same phase. The two unmarked steps differ
by $\delta$ (source~77-44's $\Delta$), forcing $u^\delta=1$: for odd $r$,
$u=1$; for even $r$ all exponents are odd, so $u=\pm1$ gives the same
conclusion. Then every mark equals $1$. Compactness gives a strict uniform
gap away from that mark point.
\end{proof}
Source~77-44 states the lemma for $r=4,5$ and extends it to every fixed
row in its Theorem~6. With the uniform square-root expansions near
$\boldsymbol1$ (Section~\ref{fce:sec:saddle}), the lemma makes the mark
integral outside a fixed neighbourhood of $\boldsymbol1$ exponentially
smaller than the main term, which is what Section~6.4 provides by
continuation. The operator bound needs no continuation in $t$ and no
analysis of colliding roots.

\section{Literature pointers and further remarks}\label{fce:ba:sec:literature}
Source~77-44 adds two references to those of the other sources. Denisov and
Wachtel \cite{dw} give a much more general local-limit framework for random
walks in cones, compatible with the exponent; ``its existence theorem alone
does not replace the explicit constant calculation or all-order argument''.
Banderier, Krattenthaler, Krinik, Kruchinin, Kruchinin, Nguyen and Wallner
\cite{basketball}, Section~3.3 and Theorem~3.8, already give univariate
basketball-walk asymptotics and explain the computation of all higher
Puiseux orders; their statements fix length and endpoints, not equal
multiplicities of every step, so the mark extraction and its global-arc
control are the additional ingredients. In the five-letter case deletion and
reinsertion of the zero steps reduces the four nonzero steps to basketball
steps; this is the reduction \eqref{fce:kk:eq:zeroinsert} of Part~IV.
``These comparisons do not constitute a comprehensive publication-priority
claim.'' The OEIS leading constants ``are prior statements, not new
conjectures''.

\begin{remark}[A nested-logarithm inverse for every row (source~77-44)]\label{fce:ba:rem:inverse}
Write the expansion as $A_r(n)=K_re^{\lambda n}n^{-\beta}(1+\alpha_{r,1}/n+\cdots)$
with $\lambda=r\log r$, $\beta=(r+1)/2$, and fix $J$. Source~77-44 defines
the \emph{chosen analytic model}
$F_J(x)=K_re^{\lambda x}x^{-\beta}(1+\sum_{j\le J}\alpha_{r,j}x^{-j})$ for
$x>X_J$, positive and strictly increasing for large $X_J$, so that
$F_J^{-1}(A_r(n))=n+O_J(n^{-J-1})$. With $L=\log Y$ and
$B=\beta\log\lambda+\log K_r$ its compatible formal inverse begins
\begin{equation}\label{fce:ba:eq:loginverse}
 x=\frac1\lambda\left[L+\beta\log L-B
 +\frac{\beta^2\log L-\beta B-\lambda\alpha_{r,1}}{L}
 +O\!\left(\frac{(\log L)^2}{L^2}\right)\right].
\end{equation}
This extends \eqref{fce:lg:eq:loginverse} by one order, for every row; for
$r=5$ it is consistent with \eqref{fce:kk:eq:loganchor} combined with
\eqref{fce:eq:inversefive}. The discrete threshold is localized by rounding
an interval of width $O_J(x^{-J-1})$ around $F_J^{-1}(Y)$; exact rounding
requires separation from an integer at that accuracy, and ``no uniform
exact-rounding claim is made''. As for the other sources, the core is the
large-solution case $b<0$ of \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} and the
corrections are \texttt{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion} of the transseries
volume (Section~\ref{fce:sec:repository}); the threshold step is
\texttt{p0:thm:staircase}. No novelty is claimed.
\end{remark}
The intake checked \eqref{fce:ba:eq:loginverse} by substituting it into
$\lambda x-\beta\log x+\log K_r+\alpha_{r,1}/x=L$: the constant and the
$L^{-1}$ terms cancel.

\section{Source 77-44's computations and questions}\label{fce:ba:sec:checks}
Source~77-44 ships an exact checker, five derivation scripts and their
receipts (Appendix~\ref{fce:app:package}). \path{code/77-44-bal-verify_ballot_kernels.py}
enumerates all count vectors in the box $0\le b_i\le3$, checks the
bridge--excursion coefficient identity for both step sets and the
degree-six equation for the five-step set with no nonzero residuals, and
outputs the diagonals $(1,7,403,40350)$ and $(1,35,18720,19369350)$, which
agree with the entries. \path{code/77-44-bal-verify_ballot_large_dp.py}
recomputes the two diagonals through $n=30$ by a rolling-array dynamic
program; for five letters it removes and reinserts the zero steps. The
first-correction scripts use exact symbolic arithmetic, and
\path{code/77-44-bal-check.py} checks the correction formula identities, the
generic degree-six identity, the covariance determinants for
$q=2,\ldots,16$ and 134 finite bridge identities. ``These numerical checks,
and numerical values from the OEIS, are diagnostics, not part of the
proof.''

At placement the intake reran three of the scripts on copies: the kernel
check, the dynamic program and the numerical-derivative check reproduced
their receipts (modulo line endings), and the two first-correction scripts
printed the values of \eqref{fce:ba:eq:BS} and of $\alpha_{5,1}$. The
checker \path{code/77-44-bal-check.py} and the driver
\path{code/77-44-bal-verify.py} did not finish within three minutes on a
loaded machine and were not rerun; the delivered receipts record their
passes. The diagonals through $n=30$ equal
\path{data/61-balanced-exact_rows.json} and
\path{data/75-03-ballot-words-a215570_terms.csv} on every common index.

Source~77-44's questions are those of Section~\ref{fce:sec:future}:
numerical effective remainder constants (``Determine sharp asymptotic error
constants''); proving the particular guessed recurrence (resolved for
A215570 by Part~\ref{fce:kk:part}; open for A215562); ``a genuinely
exponentially improved transseries or a single selected exact analytic
interpolation'' (``Determine the exponentially small sectors''); and an
alphabet size growing with $n$ (``Let the alphabet grow''). ``None of these
is silently inferred'' from its theorems. With $\alpha_{4,1}$, the target of
Section~\ref{fce:sec:future} (``Automate all-order coefficient extraction'')
is met for $r=4$ at first order; $\alpha_{4,2}$, $\alpha_{4,3}$ and the rows
$r=6,7,8$ remain open.

%% Part VI (written 2026-10-05, batch 101): bundle Reports 90 and 86
%% (sources 101-90 and 101-86).  All labels of this Part carry the
%% prefix fce:dx:.  It follows Part V, so no earlier number moves.
%% ====================================================================
\part{Direct excursion proofs: every fixed alphabet, and the second correction of A215570 without the recurrence}\label{fce:dx:part}

Part~VI was added on October~5, 2026, in batch~101 of ProveIt's
incoming-reports intake, from two reports of one session bundle (bundle
Reports~90 and~86, here sources~101-90 and~101-86), after Part~V.
Source~101-90 proves Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:main} and the D-finiteness part of
Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:class} for every fixed alphabet, in marker coordinates
that leave the two \emph{extreme} steps unmarked; source~101-86 proves them,
and Theorem~\ref{fce:thm:five}, for the five-letter row after deleting the
zero steps. They give a \emph{fifth} and a \emph{sixth} proof of results this
report already has (after sources~61, 42, 75-03 and~77-44), and those
results are not printed again: Table~\ref{fce:dx:tab:overlap} lists them and
Table~\ref{fce:dx:tab:map} maps the second routes. What the two reports add
is printed in full: source~101-86's computation of $\alpha_{5,2}$
\emph{without} the recurrence of Part~IV, with every intermediate value
(Theorem~\ref{fce:dx:thm:alpha52}); the leading constant of the zero-free
count $A^\circ_5(n)$ (Proposition~\ref{fce:dx:prop:Bn}); the inequality
$A_5(n+1)\ge35A_5(n)$ for every $n$ (Proposition~\ref{fce:dx:prop:supermult},
generalized at this write); source~101-90's zero-step identity for every odd
alphabet (Proposition~\ref{fce:dx:prop:zero}), its closed determinant
(Proposition~\ref{fce:dx:prop:det}) and its account of the periodic torus
points (Lemma~\ref{fce:dx:lem:torus}). The rest of the report is unchanged
apart from the title page and abstract, paragraphs added to
Sections~\ref{fce:sec:sources} and~\ref{fce:sec:choices} and to the
conclusion (Section~23), dated notes that point here, rows added to
Tables~\ref{fce:tab:crosswalk} and~\ref{fce:tab:renamed}, the merged research
questions of Part~III and the package description of
Appendix~\ref{fce:app:package}; no existing label or number was changed.
Statements and proofs supplied by this write rather than by a source are
marked \textup{[write]}.

\section{Provenance, overlap and conventions of Part~VI}\label{fce:dx:sec:intro}
\subsection{Two bundle reports}\label{fce:dx:sec:source}
The two reports arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} as archives of a
177-archive session bundle and were placed in commit \texttt{f7c612c72}
(batch~101).
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Source 101-90} (bundle Report~90): \emph{Balanced ballot words
over every fixed alphabet: exact constants, all algebraic orders, and
inversion}, archive \path{A215561_Fixed_Alphabet_Asymptotics.zip}
(248{,}562 bytes; a 474-line source with 24 labels and an 8-page US-letter
PDF). It is the base of this Part, because it treats every fixed $r$.
\item \textbf{Source 101-86} (bundle Report~86): \emph{Balanced five-letter
ballot words: an all-orders asymptotic and its inverse}, archive
\path{A215570_Balanced_Ballot_Asymptotics.zip} (246{,}773 bytes; a 433-line
source with 21 labels and a 7-page US-letter PDF). It is the case $r=5$ in
other coordinates, with one computation the others lack.
\end{itemize}
Both are dated October~1, 2026, with the author line ``Research note''.
Neither names a ProveIt commit or path, and neither records a repository
search. Their PDFs were created at 09:37 (101-86) and 10:16 PDT (101-90),
about nine hours
before this report's first placement (\texttt{6e193dd4f}, 19:16 PDT), so
neither could have seen any part of it. They come from one session (an
identical build script; $4.7$\,\% and $4.2$\,\% of their 8-word shingles are
common, mostly boilerplate); against this report only 1.3\,\% (101-86) and 1.5\,\%
(101-90) of their shingles occur, so there is no textual copying. They
describe their novelty against the OEIS and the literature only, in the same
words: a bounded primary-source check ``is a source-relative statement, not
an exhaustive priority claim'' (101-86) and ``is not an exhaustive worldwide
priority claim'' (101-90).

Neither report says anything about AI assistance. Their receipts record a
pre-delivery approval of the producing pipeline (in source~101-86's words,
``Root and an independent research reviewer approved the complete proof and
final transcription'') and
an ``independent'' review file each; these are self-reported reviews, not
refereeing (Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:checks}). Both are unrefereed, and no Lean
or Rocq development checks any statement of this Part. Their programs and
data ship with the prefixes \texttt{101-90-alphabet-} and
\texttt{101-86-five-} (Section~\ref{fce:dx:sec:checks},
Appendix~\ref{fce:app:package}).

The two reports keep these limitations, which this Part keeps with them.
Source~101-90: the alphabet is fixed and ``uniformity when $r$ grows is a
separate problem''; D-finiteness is an existence statement, and ``no
particular guessed recurrence'' or minimal order is identified; no smaller
exponential sectors and no explicit numerical remainder constants; root
products for larger $r$ are ``orientation only''; its code checks examples
and is ``not a general-purpose implementation''; and ``no assertion that
their smallest field is $\Q(\gamma_r)$ is needed''. Source~101-86: its
expansion is a Poincar\'e expansion of the dominant exponential sector,
``not a complete description of every smaller exponential contribution'';
it does not use, and does not prove, the order-three recurrence; its inverse
keeps its integer-rounding qualification, with ``no numerical error constant
or unconditional exact-ceiling rule'' and no exponentially improved error;
a single ceiling is certified only away from shrinking neighbourhoods of
integers; its finite checks ``supplement the analytic proof''; and the
kernel method is classical \cite{bf}.

Parts~I--V already contain, and Table~\ref{fce:dx:tab:map} maps their proofs
of Theorems~\ref{fce:thm:main}--\ref{fce:thm:five} beside the earlier ones.
They are second routes: the theorems were proved, four times, before the two
reports were written, and no novelty is claimed for them here.

\subsection{Conventions of Part~VI}\label{fce:dx:sec:conventions}
Part~VI uses the merged notation of Section~\ref{fce:sec:notation}, and
Table~\ref{fce:dx:tab:renamed} lists every renamed symbol of the two reports.
Two new coordinate systems appear, each with its own phase and Hessian:
\emph{extreme-unmarked} coordinates (source~101-90), which mark the $r-2$
interior steps and leave $\pm h$ unmarked, carry the subscript ``ex''; the
\emph{zero-free} coordinates of source~101-86, which mark two statistics of
the four nonzero five-letter steps, carry the subscript ``$\circ$''. Both
reports use the unnormalized step polynomial $\sum_iu^{s_i}$, as source~77-44
does, so their critical times are $1/r$ and $1/4$, not $1$. No other
normalization changed.
